# THE BOOK OF TOBIT

->*NEUBAUER*<-

<term>The Book of Tobit, Neubauer</term> <note>Adolf Neubauer (1831–1907) was a prominent scholar and sub-librarian at the Bodleian Library. He is best known in this context for his 1878 publication of a unique Aramaic version of the Book of Tobit, which he discovered among medieval manuscripts. The Book of Tobit itself is a popular work of the Apocrypha, telling the story of a devout Israelite living in Nineveh.</note>

# PREFACE.

Jerome, in his preface to the translation of Tobit, says that he translated it from a Chaldee <note>A term historically used by scholars to refer to Aramaic</note> text. We give his own words<sup>1</sup>:

> To Bishops Chromatius and Heliodorus, Jerome the Priest [sends] greeting in the Lord. <note>original: "Chromatio et Heliodoro Episcopis Hieronymus Presbyter in Domino salutem."</note>
> 
> I do not cease to wonder at the persistence of your demand: for you demand that I translate a book written in the Chaldean language into the Latin style—the book of Tobit, indeed, which the Hebrews, cutting it out from the catalog of Divine Scriptures, have relegated to those writings they call Apocrypha. I have satisfied your desire, though not my own inclination. For the scholarship of the Hebrews reproaches us and blames us for translating these books for Latin ears against their own canon. But judging it better to displease the judgment of the Pharisees <note>Jerome refers here to the Jewish religious authorities of his time</note> and to serve the orders of Bishops, I persisted as best I could. And because the language of the Chaldeans is closely related to Hebrew speech, I found a speaker very skilled in both languages and undertook the labor of a single day: whatever he expressed to me in Hebrew words, I—having summoned a scribe—set forth in Latin words. <note>original: "et quidquid ille mihi Hebraicis verbis expressit, hoc ego, accito notario, sermonibus Latinis exposui."</note> I shall count your prayers as the reward for this work, when I learn that I have completed what you deigned to command to your satisfaction.

Since his time nothing had been heard of a Chaldee text of Tobit; no critic, however, doubted the veracity of the

---

<sup>1</sup> Works, edited by Vallarsi, Verona, 1740, volume 10. <note>original: "Opera, ed. Vallarsi, Verona, 1740, t. x."</note>

# PREFACE.

<warning>The right margin of the page is cut off, resulting in incomplete words at the ends of many lines in both the main text and the footnotes.</warning>

father’s statement. The text which we now publish agrees in one important point with the version of the Vulgate <note>The 4th-century Latin translation of the Bible</note> in representing Tobit in the first chapters in the third person, whilst in all other old versions he speaks in the first person. It is true, however, that our Chaldee <term>Chaldee|A term historically used by scholars to refer to the Aramaic language</term> text is less in accordance with the Vulgate than with the other texts, as we shall point out later on, and in many places it differs in order and words from Jerome’s translation. This, however, can be accounted for. On the one hand, there are many omissions in our manuscript of the Chaldee text<sup>1</sup>, and it has most likely been abridged for adaptation to the Midrash <term>Midrash|A Jewish method of interpreting or expanding upon biblical stories</term>. On the other hand, Jerome, who translated his text in one day with the help of a **Jewish** interpreter, dictating it at the same time in Latin to his secretary, could hardly have made an accurate translation. Moreover, he made use, as is evident, of the old Latin version, called the *Itala*; and it cannot be doubted that he revised his translation before giving it to the public. His method in translating Tobit, although he does not mention it, was probably the same as that which he employed in the translation of Judith from a Chaldee text. We qu<unclear>ote</unclear> a part of his preface to that book<sup>2</sup>: 

> “Among the Hebrews, the Book of Judith is read among the Apocrypha <note>Non-canonical religious writings</note>: its authority for strengthening those things which come into dispute is judged less suitable. Written however in the Chaldean language, it is counted among histories. But because the Council of Nicaea <note>The first ecumenical council of the Christian Church, held in 325 AD</note> is said to have counted this book in the number of Holy Scriptures...” <note>original: “Apud Hebraeos Liber Judith inter Apocrypha legitur: cujus auctoritas ad roboranda illa quae in contentionem veniunt, minus idonea judicatur. Chaldeo tamen sermone conscriptus, inter historias computatur. Sed quia hunc librum Synodus Nicaena in numero sanctarum Scripturarum legitur computasse”</note>

***

<sup>1</sup> See pp. 6, 7, 8, 9, and 15. On p. 8, lines 3–5, the text is even out of order.
<sup>2</sup> Works, vol. x. Vallarsi <note>Domenico Vallarsi (1702–1771), a noted Italian scholar and editor of Jerome’s works</note> already says, “He perhaps followed the same method by which he had just prepared the version of Tobit, so that while another person rendered into Hebrew what was written in Chaldee, he himself recast it into Latin, following the historical truth more carefully than the sequence of sentences and words.” <note>original: “Eo fortasse, quo Tobiae versionem modo adornaverat, ut quae Chaldaice scripta erant, alio in Hebraicum reddente, ipse in Latinum refunderet, historiae magis veritatem quam sententiarum et verborum seriem sedulo persequutus.”</note> See also Bickel in the *Journal for Catholic Theology* <note>original: “Zeitschrift für Katholische Theologie”</note>, 1878, ii, p. 221.

->vii<-

# PREFACE. vii

“I have yielded to your request, or rather your demand; and putting aside the duties by which I was heavily burdened, I have devoted one short night's work <note>original: *lucubratiunculam* — literally "a little work by lamplight"</note> to this, translating sense from sense rather than word for word. I have cut away the very faulty variety of many manuscripts; I have expressed in Latin only those things which I could find with sound understanding in the Chaldee <term>Chaldee|Aramaic</term> words¹.”

Accordingly, if we take into consideration the somewhat arbitrary proceedings of the Rabbi who adapted his text to the Midrash <term>Midrash|Jewish interpretive storytelling</term>, and of Jerome who paid more attention to the sense than to the words, and who evidently made many additions (e.g., ii. 12—19, iii. 16—23, vi. 17 to end), we may venture to say that our Chaldee text in a more complete form was the original from which the translation of the Vulgate <note>The 4th-century Latin Bible</note> was made.

Before entering into details on the old versions of Tobit and their relations to the Chaldee text, we must give some account of our MS <note>Manuscript</note>. It was bought at Constantinople by Herr Fischel Hirsch, bookseller at Halberstadt, and purchased from him for the Bodleian Library <note>The main research library of the University of Oxford</note>. It contains a collection of smaller and larger Midrashim <note>plural of Midrash</note> ², copied in the fifteenth century in Greek-rabbinical characters <note>A style of Hebrew handwriting used by Jewish communities in the Byzantine or Greek-speaking regions</note>. The book of Tobit is the fifth piece of this collection, and is stated to be an extract from the *Midrash Rabbah de Rabbah* <note>The "Greatest Great Midrash"</note>. We know the *Midrash Rabbah* or *Rabboth* on the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible</note> and the five Scrolls <note>The books of Song of Songs, Ruth, Lamentations, Ecclesiastes, and Esther</note>, of which that on Genesis is—

---

¹ We take the opportunity of correcting Dr. Jellinek's statement in *Beth ham-Midrash* <note>The House of Study, a collection of midrashic texts</note> (vol. i, p. xxiii), that the first Hebrew translation of the book of Judith was made by R. 'Aqiba Levi in 1679. There are two earlier translations of this book: 1. A literal one of the text of the Vulgate, made before 1547 A.D., to be found in the MS. Opp. 712 <note>Part of the collection of David Oppenheimer</note> in the Bodleian Library (see our Catalogue, No. 2240). 2. A less literal one, printed at Venice, about 1650 (see Steinschneider's *Catal. Bodl.* <note>Catalogue of the Bodleian</note>, No. 1340, and [Zedner's] *Catalogue of the British Museum*, p. 149).

² See our *Catalogue of the Bodleian Hebrew MSS.*, No. 2339.

attributed to Rabbi Oshaya¹ <note>A 3rd-century scholar in Israel</note>, but no mention whatever is made of a *Great Midrash of the Great* <note>original: "Midrash Rabbah de Rabbah"</note>, either in old or in modern works on Jewish literature. Raymund Martini <note>A 13th-century Dominican friar and polemicist</note>, however, gives in his *Dagger of Faith*² <note>original: "Pugio Fidei"</note> a large number of extracts from a *Great Genesis Midrash* <note>original: "Midrash B'reshith major"</note>, and amongst them a part of the history of Bel and the Dragon <note>An ancient Jewish story often added to the Book of Daniel</note>, agreeing *word for word* <note>original: "verbatim"</note> with the text here published from our manuscript.³ In our manuscript, it is said to be extracted from the *Great Midrash of the Great*. It is certain, therefore, that the *Great Genesis Midrash* used by Martini and our *Great Midrash of the Great* are the same book. Don Isaac Abarbanel⁴ <note>A famous Portuguese Jewish statesman and philosopher (1437–1508)</note>, who seems to have possessed a copy of this *Great Midrash*, quotes it under the name of the *Greater Great Genesis Midrash* <note>original: "B'reshith Rabbah Rabbathâ"</note> or simply the *Great Genesis Midrash*⁵. According to our manuscript, however, we must call it the *Midrash Rabbah of Rabbah*, meaning it is attributed to the scholar Rabbah⁶. Martini also gives many extracts from a *Great Genesis Midrash* attributed to Rabbi Moses the Preacher <note>original: "R. Moses had-Darshan"</note>, which the scholar Zunz⁷ thinks is the same as the already-mentioned *Great Genesis Midrash*. They are, however, in our opinion, two different books, for the following reasons: 1. We give on page 36 of the text an additional passage to the *Midrash Tanhuma* <note>A collection of homiletic teachings</note>, which is attributed to Rabbi Moses the Preacher, referring to the history of Tobit, though no names are given. Now this piece has little in

# PREFACE.

<b>b</b>

common with our text of Tobit extracted from the anonymous **Great Midrash** <note>original: "Midrash major"</note>. 2. Don Isaac Abarbanel <note>A prominent Jewish statesman and philosopher (1437–1508)</note>, as we have stated, possessed a copy of the **Great Midrash**, but when he quotes the extracts from the **Great Genesis** <note>original: "B'reshith Rabbah"</note> of **Rabbi Moses the Preacher** <note>original: "R. Moses had-Darshan," an 11th-century scholar from Narbonne</note>, he adds that he cannot verify the quotation. Martini <note>Raymund Martini, a 13th-century Dominican friar</note> had therefore, no doubt, two **Midrashic collections** <note>original: "Midrashim"</note> furnished him by the Jews, either in two distinct manuscripts or in one, where the text was the **Great Midrash** and the marginal notes or additions were by **Rabbi Moses the Preacher**. This last was the case with the manuscript of the **Great Midrash** <note>original: "Midrash Rabbathi"</note>, formerly in possession of the celebrated Rapoport¹ <note>Solomon Judah Loeb Rapoport, a pioneering scholar of the Jewish Enlightenment</note>, and now in the library of the Jewish congregation at Prague, and which Dr. Jellinek describes as the work of **Rabbi Moses the Preacher**².

Our **Aramaic** <note>original: "Chaldee"</note> text quoted from the **Great Midrash of Rabbah** <note>original: "Midrash Rabbah of Rabbah"</note>, which we have identified with the **Major Great Genesis** <note>original: "B'reshith Rabbah major"</note> of Martini, would, even if we were to accept Zunz's³ <note>Leopold Zunz, the founder of modern historical Jewish studies</note> identification of it with the **Great Genesis** of **Rabbi Moses the Preacher**, be known at all events at the beginning of the eleventh century amongst the Jews. It must, however, be much older (as might have been argued from our distinction between the two **Midrashic collections**, namely the anonymous **Great Midrash** and that of **Rabbi Moses the Preacher**), since it is anterior to the Hebrew text published by us, which is believed to be from the fifth to the seventh century⁴. For the Hebrew cannot be a translation from—

# PREFACE.

the Old Latin version <note>original: "Itala"</note>, as has been hitherto believed¹, but must be from an Aramaic <note>original: "Chaldee"</note> text similar to ours, in which, however, Tobit speaks throughout in the first person². We conclude, therefore, that Jerome <note>The 4th-century scholar responsible for the Latin Vulgate Bible</note> had our present Aramaic text in a fuller form before him when he made his translation of the book.

We have now to make brief mention of the various old texts of the book of Tobit and their relations to one another. We shall follow entirely Professor Bickell’s excellent article on the subject³. **a.** There are three Greek versions: 1. The one usually found in all manuscripts, which we shall call **A**; 2. The Sinaitic text⁴, called **B**; 3. A later text, of which only large fragments exist⁵, which we shall call **C**. ***β***. Of Latin translations, the earlier is the Old Latin <note>original: "Itala"</note>, of which three⁶ versions exist, varying considerably one from the other: ***a***. The complete text published by Pierre Sabatier⁷ <note>A French Benedictine scholar (1682–1742) known for his work on the Old Latin Bible</note>, which we reproduce in full, this book not being accessible to every biblical scholar; ***b***. That edited by Giuseppe Bianchini⁸ <note>An 18th-century Italian biblical scholar</note>;

---

Leipzig 1853, p. 14. The Hebrew translation published by Fagius <note>Paul Fagius, a 16th-century Hebraist</note> from the edition of Constantinople, 1519, is certainly much more recent than the Münster text <note>The Hebrew version published by Sebastian Münster in 1542</note>. Not only from the artificial style ought Professor Fritsche to have known that, but also from passages like **"until I went into Germany"** <note>original Hebrew: "צד [עד] שהלכתי באלמנייא"</note> (ii. 11) and the play upon the words **"last brother"** <note>original Hebrew: "אחי אחרון"</note> for **Achiacharus** <note>original Greek: "'Αχιάχαρος"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the editor’s technical analysis of the various versions of the Book of Tobit, moving from the Old Latin manuscripts to the significance of the Aramaic (Chaldee) and Hebrew texts included in this edition.</meta>

-># PREFACE.<-

c. The fragments published by Cardinal Angelo Mai¹. **γ.** The Vulgate is the later translation by Jerome <note>The standard Latin Bible of the Catholic Church</note>, which was made from an Aramaic <note>original: "Chaldee"</note> text; the translator made extensive use of the Old Latin <note>original: "Itala"</note>². **δ.** Our Aramaic <note>original: "Chaldee"</note> text agrees for the most part with the Sinaitic Greek text <note>The 4th-century Codex Sinaiticus</note>, and consequently with the Old Latin. However, the Aramaic text has sentences which are found sometimes in one and sometimes in another of the above-mentioned versions³; others are unique to the Aramaic text or the Hebrew translation. This fact alone would be sufficient to show that the Aramaic is not a translation from one of the Greek or Latin versions⁴; and moreover, the pure Semitic style <note>original: "idiom"</note> of the Aramaic text does not admit for a moment the possibility of it being a translation from a non-Semitic text⁵. **ε.** The Hebrew text which we publish is a translation from an earlier version <note>original: "recension"</note> of our Aramaic text, which preserved the more original form—namely, Tobit speaking in the first person. In fact, many omissions...

-># PREFACE.<-

and not a few corruptions in our Aramaic <note>original: "Chaldee"</note> text, which is published from a single manuscript, can be supplied and amended from the Hebrew translation; however, the Hebrew translation has additions by the copyists, such as the enlargement of the prayers and Midrashic <term>Midrashic: relating to Midrash, a Jewish method of interpreting or elaborating upon biblical texts to draw out legal, social, or ethical meanings</term> application of biblical verses. Both the Aramaic and Hebrew versions leave out the mention of the dog; nor do they contain the latter part of the thirteenth or any portion of the fourteenth chapter. The latter omissions, however, are also to be found in a Syriac text based on a Greek version, and in a manuscript of the Old Latin <note>original: "Itala"</note>¹. The Hebrew text, although shorter, agrees in the last chapters partly with the Sinaitic text <note>The 4th-century Codex Sinaiticus, one of the oldest bibles</note>.

We publish the Hebrew text from the first edition printed at Constantinople in 1516, which Sebastian Münster reproduced in 1542, and which is hence usually but wrongly called the Münster text, as if he had been the first who published it. This text is marked in our notes with **M**. The same text, with Münster’s Latin version, is to be found in Walton’s Polyglot <term>Polyglot: a Bible containing the same text in several languages side-by-side</term>. -># PREFACE. xiv<-

The **Greater Genesis Rabbah** <note>original: "B'reshith Rabbah major," a significant collection of Jewish rabbinic commentary on the first book of the Bible</note> is distinct from that of **Rabbi Moses the Preacher** <note>original: "R. Moses had-Darshan," an 11th-century scholar from Narbonne</note>. For this reason, we have also thought it necessary to add in an appendix the history of **Bel and the Dragon** in the **Syriac** <note>An ancient Aramaic dialect used by Eastern Christians and some Jewish communities</note> version¹, which is given in the manuscript as extracted from the **Great Midrash** <note>original: "Midrash Rabbah"</note> of Rabbah; part of this, as we have already stated, is quoted in the *Dagger of Faith* <note>original: "Pugio Fidei," a 13th-century Latin work by Raymund Martini used in theological debates</note> as being taken from the "Greater Midrash." The Syriac version of the **Apocrypha** <note>Ancient religious texts not included in the standard Hebrew Bible but found in some Christian versions</note>, transcribed in Hebrew characters, was known among the Jews in Spain. **Moses ben Nahman of Gerona**, usually known as **Nahmanides**, quotes in his commentary on the **Five Books of Moses** <note>original: "Pentateuch"</note> passages of the Syriac version of the **Wisdom of Solomon**, and the *Scroll of Shushan* <note>original: "M'gillath Shushan"</note>, or the **Book of Judith**². To this we have annexed a small passage of the **Genesis Rabbah** <note>original: "B'reshith Rabbah"</note>, or the **Great Midrash** on Genesis, which alludes to the history of **Bel and the Dragon**. We may mention, however, that this passage is not to be found in some manuscripts of this Midrash, a statement which will be useful for the defense of **Raymund Martini** against recent attacks upon him, which follows as a note to this preface.

We have now to say a word about the language in which the original book of **Tobit** was composed. That the author wrote in a **Semitic** dialect cannot be doubted; the earliest translations sufficiently prove this³. Written by a Jew, we have only to choose between the **Hebrew** of the later idiom and **Aramaic** <note>original: "Chaldee," an older term for the Aramaic language family</note>, from which last our text would be abridged and adapted to the <term>Midrash: a Jewish method of interpreting or elaborating upon biblical texts to draw out legal, social, or ethical meanings</term>. We agree here again with **Dr. Bickell**⁴, that the original composition—

...of the book was in Hebrew, although no such text is mentioned by **Origen** <note>An influential early Christian scholar and theologian (c. 184–253 AD) who compiled the Hexapla</note> and his contemporaries. Indeed, proper names like **Rafael**, that is, 'may God heal'¹ (with allusion to the double healing of **Tobit** and **Sarah**), **Tobi** and—

---

—and that the **Chaldean** <note>Aramaic</note> version read "week" <note>original: שבוע (shavu'a)</note> for it and translated accordingly (p. 11, l. 4) "her clothing" <note>original: לבושה (levushah)</note>, "you shall pity" <note>original: תחוס (takhus)</note>, since this error would be impossible in the corresponding passage on p. 12, l. 12. "To **Baal**" <note>original Greek: Τῇ Βάαλ</note> in chapter 1, verse 5 of the Greek version **A** is a corruption for **Bethel** <note>literally "House of God"; text, p. 3, l. 12</note>. Another argument for a Hebrew original of **Tobit** is brought forward by **Professor Graetz** (*History of the Jews*, 2nd edition, vol. iv, p. 466), and literally translated by **Dr. Ginsburg** (in Kitto’s *Cyclopedia*, article on "Tobit"), from the passage in chapter 4, verse 17, "Pour out your bread upon the grave of the righteous" <note>original Greek: Ἔκχεον τοὺς ἄρτους σου ἐπὶ τὸν τάφον τῶν δικαίων; Aramaic, p. 8, l. 10; Hebrew, p. 24, l. 9</note>, which, they say, could only be explained if we suppose the original had "send forth thy bread amongst the righteous" <note>original: שלח לחמך בקרב הצדיקים (shlakh lakhmecha be-kerev ha-tzadikim)</note>; 'the translator (Dr. Ginsburg says) by a transposition of the last two letters having read "in the grave" <note>original: בקבר (be-kever)</note> instead of "amongst" <note>original: בקרב (be-kerev)</note>, and "poured out" <note>original: שפך (shafakh)</note> instead of "sent forth" <note>original: שלח (shlakh)</note>, as is evident from the contrasting clause, "and give it not to the wicked."' 

The two authors might have mentioned the text of the **Old Latin** <note>Itala b</note> version: "Distribute your bread and wine with the righteous" <note>original Latin: 'Panem tuum et vinum distribue cum justis'</note>. The correction is, in our opinion, not necessary at all. In ancient times it was already customary for the friends of mourners to bring them food and drink; compare **2 Samuel 3:35** (**Ewald**, *The Antiquities of the People of Israel*, 3rd ed., p. 204). **Sirach** (30:18) and **Josephus** (*The Jewish War*, II. i. 1) allude to the same custom (**Fritsche**, op. cit., p. 46, and **Perles**, in Frankel's *Monthly Journal*, vol. x, p. 394). 

The **Talmud** <note>The central collection of Jewish rabbinic law and tradition</note> also mentions similar usages (**S'mahoth**, chapter 14, and the **Jerusalem Talmud**, **Berakhot** 3:5). The **meal of mourning** <note>original: סעודת הבראה (se’udat havra’ah), the 'meal of restoration' served to the bereaved after a funeral</note> after the burial, which is still in use among the Jews, is no doubt a remnant of the ancient rite (private communication of Dr. Perles); for "poured out" <note>original: שפך (shafakh)</note> as well as "to pour out" <note>original Greek: ἐκχέειν</note> in the sense of 'giving freely,' see Fritsche, op. cit., p. 45, and the **Babylonian Talmud**, **Erubin**, folio 65a. **Jerome’s** <note>Translator of the Latin Vulgate (c. 347–420 AD)</note> translation, "provide bread" <note>original Latin: 'constitue panem'</note> etc., would imply the reading "I will set" <note>original: אשוי (ashvey)</note> instead of "I will behold" <note>original: אשור (ashur)</note> of our text. 

The correction of **Mitzandon** <note>original: מצנדון; a proposed name for a region in Persia</note>, proposed by **Dr. Kohut** (**Geiger’s** *Jewish Journal*, 1872, p. 55) for "**Egypt**" <note>original: מצרים (Mitzrayim)</note> (p. 12, l. 13, and p. 29, l. 16), cannot be admitted; see p. xvii. The problem could perhaps be solved if we could guess what word the original might have had for **handwritten bond** <note>original Greek: χειρόγραφον (chirographon); Latin: chirographum</note> (chapters 5 and 9), where the Aramaic and the Hebrew texts have 'bag.' Dr. Perles proposes either "writing/stylus" <note>original: חרט (heret)</note>, which means 'writing' (**Isaiah 8:1**) as well as 'bag' (**2 Kings 5:23**), or the Talmudic "pouch" <note>original: דיסקיא (diskayya)</note> (see **Levy’s** *New Hebrew and Chaldean Lexicon*, i. 400 and ii. 169, 170).

¹ See **Fagius'** <note>Paul Fagius (1504–1549), a German Renaissance Hebraist</note> preface to his edition of **Tobit** (p. x, note 1).

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the original language of the Book of Tobit. xvi

->### PREFACE.<-

**Tobiyyah** <note>original: טוֹבִיָּה (Tobiyyah), meaning 'The LORD is good'; the author notes the name's structure is specific to later Hebrew usage</note> (the former only used in later Hebrew), **Gabaiel**, ‘treasurer of God,’ **Reuel**, and **Ednah** in connection with **Sarah**, could only be employed by a writer in Hebrew. Most of the **Apocryphal** <term>Apocryphal|Referring to a collection of ancient Jewish writings that are not included in the standard Hebrew Bible but are found in some Christian versions of the Old Testament</term> books of the Old Testament, it has now been proved by critics, were composed in Hebrew¹. No books are more subject to additions, alterations, and various adaptations than popular histories; the text is in the hands of a few, and the contents are related orally to the people; hence the great variety in the texts, even of the early translations.

As to the object of the beautiful story of **Tobit**, it is in our opinion neither an admonition to observe the payment of the tithes and to give alms, according to the view of the **Midrash** <term>Midrash|A genre of rabbinic literature which contains early interpretations and commentaries on the Hebrew Bible</term> (which **Ewald** <note>Heinrich Ewald (1803–1875), a famous German scholar of the Bible and Near Eastern languages</note> has adopted without knowing the Midrash), nor an exhortation to observe the sacrifices and other laws mentioned in **Leviticus**. Such admonitions would be nothing new, and there would be no occasion to compose a popular history to enforce them. We believe with **Prof. Graetz** and **Dr. Kohut**, that the frequent and strange allusion to a secret burial of dead men, the special demand of **Tobi** to bury him and his wife in honour, the lamentation of **Sarah** that she had no one to bury her parents, must refer to a time when this action was prohibited to the Jews. Of this we know two periods in Jewish history: 1. In the time of the domination of the **Guebres** <term>Guebres|A term for Zoroastrians in Persia; the mention refers to a time when Zoroastrian rulers might have forbidden traditional Jewish burials, as they practiced exposing the dead to the elements</term> in Persia, on which ground **Dr. Kohut**² thinks that the book was composed in Persia about the time of **Ardeshir I** <note>Also known as Artaxerxes I, the founder of the Sasanian Empire (reigned 224–242 AD)</note>. This hypothesis has in its favour that the author places the

***

¹ See for **Judith**, **F. C. Movers** in the *Journal for Philosophy and Catholic Theology* <note>original German title: 'Zeitschrift für Philosophie und Katholische Theologie'</note>, Cologne, 1835, p. 8 and following; for **Susanna**, **N. Brüll**, *Yearbooks for Jewish History* <note>original German title: 'Jahrbücher für Jüdische Geschichte'</note>, vol. iii, p. 68; for **Baruch** and minor treatises, **Plessner**, *The Apocryphal Books translated into Hebrew* <note>original German title: 'Die Apokryphischen Bücher ins Hebräische übersetzt'</note>, Berlin, 1833.

² **Geiger’s** *Jewish Journal* <note>original German title: 'Jüdische Zeitschrift'</note>, 1872, p. 70 and following.

scene of the history in Persian towns, and that **Asmodeus** <note>In Jewish folklore, the king of demons; his name is often linked to the Persian word *Aeshma-daeva*</note> and the demons are of Persian origin. But inasmuch as the book of **Tobit** is mentioned by **Clement of Alexandria** and by **Polycarp** <note>Two influential early Christian leaders from the 2nd century</note>, the time of **Ardeshir** (about 250 A.D.) <note>The founder of the Sasanian Empire in Persia</note> would be too late. 2. **Prof. Graetz**¹ puts its composition in the time of **Hadrian** <note>Roman Emperor, reigned 117–138 A.D.</note>, after the fall of the famous fortress of **Bether**, so valiantly defended by **Bar-Kokhba** <note>Leader of the ill-fated Jewish rebellion against the Roman Empire</note>. The **Talmud**² mentions in fact that the benediction after meals, 'Blessed be he who is good and doeth good,' was instituted after the dead bodies round **Bether** were allowed to be buried³. **Nineveh** and **Babel** <note>Babylon</note> in the later Jewish as also the Christian literature allude always to **Rome**. To this opinion we adhere; at all events the book can scarcely have been composed earlier, since it was not known to **Josephus** <note>A first-century Jewish historian who otherwise recorded major biblical stories</note>.

We express our best thanks to our friend Mr. **H. J. Mathews**, M. A., for the revision of the proofs, and especially for the trouble he has taken to adapt our translation to the English of the authorized version⁴. We have also to thank M. **Delisle**, Member of the French Institute and Director of the National Library in Paris, for the loan of the MS. <note>Manuscript</note> containing the Persian translation.

A. N. <note>Adolf Neubauer, the editor and translator</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the general preface into a specific scholarly defense of Raymund Martini, an important source for the texts being edited. # NOTE ON
# RAYMUND MARTINI'S DAGGER OF FAITH <note>original: "Pugio Fidei"; a 13th-century polemical work by a Dominican friar aimed at converting Jews and Muslims</note>.

WE have had to quote the *Dagger of Faith* in connection with the *Midrash* <note>A form of Jewish biblical commentary and interpretation</note> out of which the Aramaic <note>original: "Chaldee"</note> text of **Tobit** and the Syriac text of **Bel and the Dragon** are taken. In relying upon Martini's authority we are bound, contrary to our will, to defend him against the attack made lately upon him in calling him a forger and an impostor¹. At the time when **Dr. Pusey** wrote his preface to the English translation of the texts of the **Fifty-third Chapter of Isaiah** according to the Jewish Interpreters, in which he breaks a lance on behalf of the author of the *Dagger of Faith*, we had not examined the manuscript out of which we publish **Tobit**, and therefore could not provide him with the materials which we have now at our disposal. **Dr. Pusey's** defence of Martini is therefore based only on internal grounds. His conclusion is the following²:
> ‘Amid the various sources of human mistake, we are bound by the duties of our common humanity not to assume the very worst, dishonesty; but to believe what a person says that he saw with his own eyes. Enough has been said, perhaps, where demonstration on either side is impossible, since the extracts were made nearly six centuries ago, and the manuscripts which Martini had before him have long since perished.’

**Dr. Zunz**, whom **Dr. Pusey** overlooked, wrote in the same strain some forty years ago. In his unsurpassed book on the *Agadah* <note>original: "Agadah"; the non-legal, narrative, and homiletic traditions in Jewish literature</note> he speaks of **Rabbi Moses the Preacher's** <note>original: "had-Darshan"; Moses had-Darshan of Narbonne was an 11th-century scholar</note> *Midrash* and the *Dagger of Faith* in the following terms³:
> ‘**Rabbi Moses the Preacher** of **Narbonne**, the teacher of **Rabbi Nathan**⁴, and so belonging to

¹ See below, p. xx.
² *The Fifty-third Chapter of Isaiah according to the Jewish Interpreters*, Oxford, 1877, vol. ii, p. xxxv.
³ *The Liturgical Discourses of the Jews* <note>original: "Die Gottesdienstlichen Vorträge der Juden"</note>, Berlin, 1832, p. 287 sqq.
⁴ Of Rome, the celebrated author of the Talmudic Dictionary, called *Arukh* <note>original: "Arukh"; a foundational dictionary for the Talmud and Midrash, the title means "Set in Order"</note>.

It highlights that Martini likely had access to ancient manuscripts that are now lost, a view supported by scholars like Dr. Pusey and Dr. Zunz.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly investigation into the sources used by Raymund Martini. While the previous page focused on Martini’s general reputation and the defense of his character by scholars like Dr. Pusey and Dr. Zunz, this page shifts to the specific mystery of a lost Jewish commentary he cited—the Great Genesis Rabbah of Rabbi Moses the Preacher.</meta>

# NOTE ON RAYMUND MARTINI'S DAGGER OF FAITH <note>original: "Pugio Fidei"; a famous 13th-century polemical work by the Dominican monk Raymund Martini</note>.

Rabbi Moses the Preacher <note>original: "R. Moses had-Darshan"; a scholar from Narbonne, France</note>, active in the third quarter of the eleventh century, is known through various citations. He appears in the *Arukh* <note>a famous dictionary of the Talmud</note>, in the works of **Rashi** <note>the preeminent medieval commentator on the Bible and Talmud</note>, the *Thosaforth* <note>medieval "additions" or commentaries on the Talmud</note>, the *Mord'khai*, and the additions to the *Thanhumâ* <note>an ancient collection of homilies on the Torah</note>. He is recognized as the author of clarifications on Talmudic passages and various books of the Bible. His scriptural commentary provides both linguistic and factual explanations, as well as older *Agadahs* <note>homiletic or narrative teachings</note> and original interpretations in the *Agadic* style.

The study of Rabbi Moses's work is particularly complicated due to a specific circumstance. The monk **Raymund Martini**, in his *Dagger of Faith*, quotes a significant number of passages from "the Great Genesis Rabbah <note>original: "B'reshith Rabbah"; a traditional commentary on the book of Genesis</note> of Rabbi Moses the Preacher." For the most part, these passages—sometimes in the original language and sometimes in Latin translation—were later copied from Martini's work by other authors such as **Porchet**, **Joshua of Lorca**, and **Peter Galatin**. However, no such work is named by any Jewish authors. Even **Abarbanel** <note>a 15th-century statesman and philosopher</note>, who provides extracts from it, simply does so to refute the arguments of **Joshua of Lorca**, and he himself admits that he did not actually own the "Great Genesis Rabbah"¹:

This fact might make the very existence of such a *Midrash* <note>a form of Jewish biblical interpretation</note> seem suspicious, were there not strong evidence in its favor. It is not at all unusual for a work to survive only through the mentions of a single author. Furthermore, the consistent tone of the fragments and the clear indicators of their origin—which neither **Martini** nor anyone else would have known how to fake—suggest the *Midrash* was real. Many of the quoted segments are also found in other types of *Agadahs*, making it as unnecessary as it would be impossible to fabricate a work of this nature.

Finally, **Martini** was neither a convert from Judaism like **Joshua**, nor a deceiver like **Galatin**. He was a deeply learned man who did not need to resort to fraud to support his many extracts from well-known legal <note>original: "Halakhic"</note> and narrative <note>original: "Agadic"</note> writings. We must therefore accept the authenticity of that "Great Genesis Rabbah." It is left to scholars to decide whether Rabbi Moses was the sole author or if certain parts were added later. This entire investigation has taken a new turn since the actual discovery of a *Great Genesis* <note>original: "B'reshith Rabbathi"</note> in its original language². Based on the information provided to me, much of that text matches the fragments found in **Martini**, while other parts align with the known style of the author.

---

¹ See, however, above, page viii. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; ² See above, page ix.

The author argues for the work's authenticity based on Martini's scholarly character and the recent discovery of a similar manuscript.</summary>

# NOTE ON RAYMUND MARTINI'S DAGGER OF FAITH <note>original: "Pugio Fidei"; the title refers to a weapon used in religious debate</note>.

...explanations preserved by **Rashi** <note>Rabbi Shlomo Itzhaki (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval commentator on the Torah and Talmud</note>; much, however, especially the passages disputed by **Abarbanel** <note>Don Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508), a Jewish statesman and philosopher who critiqued Martini’s use of sources</note>, is missing. I am of the opinion that the work of Rabbi Moses was put forth after his time with additions under the name *The Great Genesis* <note>original: "B’reshith Rabbathi"; a specific version of the Genesis midrash associated with Rabbi Moses the Preacher</note>, and that the same was in the hands of the person who added to the *Tanhuma* <note>original: "Thanḥumâ"; an ancient collection of homilies on the Five Books of Moses</note> and perhaps **Rabbi Isaac Nathan** <note>a 15th-century scholar known for creating the first Hebrew concordance of the Bible</note>¹. To the author of the *Yalqut* <note>original: "Yalqut Shimoni"; a major collection of Jewish narrative traditions</note> it was probably only imperfectly known. In relation to this greater collection, Martini calls *our* standard *Genesis Rabbah* <note>original: "B’reshith Rabbah"</note> "the little" or "short" version.’

The Revs. A. C. Jennings and W. H. Lowe (for they claim a joint production² in the appendix which is intended to ‘triumph over a dead lion,’ as Dr. Pusey says of Martini in a private communication) are of another opinion concerning Martini. We shall quote their own words³:

> ‘The reader is warned against accepting as genuine the citations from Jewish works in Schoettgen’s *Hebrew Hours* <note>original: "Horae Hebraicae"</note> and Raymund Martini’s *Dagger of Faith*. Both works are utterly untrustworthy. Raymund Martini (Of the Order of Preachers [Dominicans] against the Moors and Jews <note>original: "Ordinis Praedicatorum adversus Mauros et Judaeos"</note>, flourished around <note>original: "fl. cir."</note> 1250) is notorious for the questionable expedients which he adopted in endeavouring to refute the Jews from their own books. With that well-meaning dishonesty which too frequently marked the controversialists of his age, he alters the text of the **Talmud**, **Midrashim**, etc., to meet his occasion, and even devises whole passages where convenient. Martini was a sound Hebrew scholar, and as his forgeries are generally clever adaptations and combinations from other parts of Hebrew literature, it is only by reference to the actual texts of these Jewish works that his impostures are betrayed.’

We do not think that any accusation of literary fraud could be expressed in stronger terms than those which the two commentators on the Psalms have chosen. Had they known Zunz’s <note>Leopold Zunz (1794–1886), the founder of the modern "Science of Judaism"</note> excellent book, they would have perhaps modified their language, and at all events not have said that Martini (we leave Schoettgen to his fate) ‘is notorious for his questionable expedients.’ Moreover, they would have learned from Zunz that the result of their ‘joint production’ is nothing new, since **Don Isaac Abarbanel**⁴, in—

# NOTE ON RAYMUND MARTINI'S DAGGER OF FAITH <note>original: "Pugio Fidei"; a famous 13th-century polemical work by the Dominican friar Raymond Martini</note>.

calling Jerome of the Holy Faith <note>original: "Hieronymus de Sancta Fide"; a Jewish physician who converted to Christianity and wrote against his former faith</note> an impostor and forger, strikes with the same blow Martini, for both quote the same passages ¹ From Abarbanel's <note>Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508), a Jewish statesman and philosopher</note> book also they could have quoted, without needing much research, many instances which would have supported their case better. On the other hand, they would have seen from Zunz <note>Leopold Zunz, the founder of the modern "Science of Judaism"</note> that the *Great Genesis* <note>original: "B'reshith Rabbah"</note> quoted by Martini is not the printed book we have, but quite a distinct work. And so they would have had no occasion to say, ‘We cannot find that the comments on verses 3 and 4, quoted in the *Dagger of Faith* as from the *Great Genesis*, have any existence in that work, nor do we believe that they were in former times discoverable there.’

But let us now take up the other passages, besides those of the *Great Genesis*, given jointly by the two learned clergymen. ‘His note,’ they say, ‘on the first verse of the Aramaic Translation <note>original: "Targum"; ancient Aramaic translations and paraphrases of the Hebrew Bible</note> of Psalm 110 is a fair sample of Martini's comments, “*Aramaic translation*: **To His word** [instead of: **By His word**] **the Lord said**... *The Lord said to His Word* <note>original: "Dixit Dominus Verbo suo"</note>... *It is highly noteworthy that the Aramaic translation says: The Lord said to His Word, where David said: The Lord said to my Lord.* [Dagger of Faith, 554. Let the student compare both Aramaic translations.]’ We confess that it is a bad case for Martini, for the phrasing ‘to His word’ <note>original: "למימריה"</note> is without example in the Aramaic translations, where we always find ‘by/in His word,’ <note>original: "במימריה"</note> ² although it is not impossible that some ignorant copyist may have written ‘to His word’ to accord with the ‘to’ of ‘to my Lord’ <note>original: "לאדני"</note>. But we are sorry to say that it is an equally bad case for the two learned authors, that they should not have seen on the margin of the *Dagger of Faith* a note by Voisin³ <note>Joseph de Voisin, a 17th-century French theologian who edited Martini's work</note>, that the Barcelona and Majorca manuscripts of this work (those are the oldest and the best) have not the passage at all, and it is therefore a later addition. How this oversight has happened to authors who state, ‘It is most unfortunate that modern commentators have so readily relied on these two authorities. In our Introduction we depend upon no passages but such as we have ourselves verified,’ we are not bound to explain.

They say further on, ‘We may instance also Martini's audacious alteration of the text of the *Book of the Rav: The Words of the Lord*’ <note>original: "Siphra d'Rab, D'boore Y'hovah"; an early rabbinic commentary on Leviticus</note>,

# NOTE ON RAYMUND MARTINI'S DAGGER OF FAITH <note>original: "Pugio Fidei"; a significant 13th-century anti-Jewish polemical work written by the Dominican friar Raymond Martini</note>.

xii. 20. We have said elsewhere¹ that the Messianic words read by Martini in the *Siphrâ* <note>one of the earliest rabbinic commentaries on the book of Leviticus</note> are not to be found either in the printed editions or in the Bodleian manuscript <note>a famous collection of historical documents at Oxford University</note>. But it is possible and probable that in the manuscripts of the book used by Martini and Jerome of the Holy Faith <note>original: "S. Fide"; a Jewish convert to Christianity who wrote against Judaism in the early 15th century</note> there was a later addition—a gloss, for instance—which eventually became part of the main text. We really do not see why Martini would have felt the need to forge a Messianic passage when he could have found the same substance elsewhere. If, however, he did quote from works that did not exist, he did so in the best of company: that of our two learned authors. They quote from a "Word of the Lord" <note>original Hebrew: דבורא יהוה, *Dibburya Yahweh*</note>, which exists neither in print nor in manuscript. Could they perhaps mean the "Word of Obligation" <note>original Hebrew: דבורא דחובא, *Dibburya de-Hobha*</note>?

Do Messrs. Jennings and Lowe believe that the Jewish scholars who were required to provide Martini with manuscripts would not have called him out for falsifying passages during their debates? This is exactly what Moses ben Naḥman of Gerona <note>also known as Nahmanides, a leading medieval Jewish scholar</note> intended to do with Paulus Christianus² when he asked him to show him the books from which he was quoting³. The books were handed over to him, and the quotations were found to be correct. Abarbanel⁴ <note>Isaac Abarbanel, a 15th-century Jewish statesman and philosopher</note> cries out against the Rabbis who were contemporaries of Jerome of the Holy Faith <note>original: "Hieronymus de Sancta Fide"</note>, asking them why they did not produce their books to show he was an impostor instead of merely arguing with him. They did so in only one instance; they did not do it in others for the simple reason that Jerome quoted correctly. That one instance involves the following passage quoted from the *Great Genesis* <note>original: "B'reshith Rabbah"; an ancient Jewish commentary on the book of Genesis</note>:

> "And Melchizedek, king of Salem <note>original: 'Et Melchizedek rex Salem'</note> (Genesis 14:18). This was Shem, the son of Noah. And what does the saying teach us when it says, *he brought forth bread and wine*? Rabbi Samuel bar Nahman said: He handed over to him the instructions of the priesthood, and he himself was sacrificing bread and wine to God, as it is written in Genesis 14:18: *And he was a priest of the Most High God*, etc." <meta>catchword: King</meta>

# NOTE ON RAYMUND MARTINI'S DAGGER OF FAITH <note>original: "Pugio Fidei"; a significant 13th-century anti-Jewish polemical work written by the Dominican friar Raymond Martini</note>.

*Salem. A place sometimes justifies its inhabitants. Alternatively: He brings forth bread* <note>original Latin: "Salem. Locus quandoque justificat habitatores suos. Aliter. Producit panem"</note>: this is the Bread of the Presence <note>original Latin: "panis propositionis"; the sacred bread kept in the Jewish Temple</note>, and the wine, that is, the drink offerings <note>original Latin: "libamina"</note>. The Masters said that he revealed the Law to him, as it is written in Proverbs 9:5: *Come, eat of my bread, and drink of the wine which I have mixed.* <note>original Latin: "Venite, comedite panem meum, et bibite vinum quod miscui"</note> Alternatively: Melchizedek—this is as if the scripture said what is written in Psalm 110:4: *The Lord has sworn and will not repent: You are a priest forever after the order of Melchizedek.* <note>original Latin: "Juravit Dominus, et non poenitebit eum; Sacerdos tu ad saeculum secundum ordinem Melchizedek"</note> And who is this? This is the righteous King and the Savior, the King Messiah, of whom it was said in Zechariah 9:9: *Behold, your king comes to you, righteous and a Savior.* <note>original Latin: "Ecce rex tuus venit tibi justus, et Salvator"</note> And what does the phrase, *He brought forth bread and wine* <note>original Latin: "Protulit panem et vinum"</note> teach? It is as if he says, *A cake of wheat upon the earth* <note>original Latin: "Placenta tritici in terra"</note>, as in Psalm 72:16. And this is what is stated in Genesis 14:18: "And he was the Priest of the most high." <note>original Latin: "Et ipse erat Sacerdos altissimi"</note> Jerome <note>Jerome of the Holy Faith, a Jewish convert to Christianity</note> has, instead of "And he himself was sacrificing the bread and wine of God" (p. xxii, l. 28), the following words: "Who was to sacrifice bread and wine." <note>original Latin: "Qui erat panem et vinum sacrificare"</note> The Rabbis contemporary with Jerome said, according to Abarbanel¹ <note>Isaac Abarbanel, a famous 15th-century Jewish philosopher and statesman</note>, that those words are not to be found in the passage quoted from the *Genesis Rabbah* <note>original: "B'reshith Rabbah"; an ancient collection of Jewish explanations on the book of Genesis</note>, but they did not deny the existence of the passage entirely. Now the two learned clergymen say with reference to the same passage: "That the *Genesis Rabbah* would unconsciously furnish arguments for the doctrines of the Immaculate Incarnation and the Eucharist is hardly probable; that Martini would be ready to father passages on *Genesis Rabbah* appears likely from his procedure elsewhere."

We have no complete copy of the "Major" *Genesis Rabbah*, but in the collection contained in our manuscript (folios 40b and 47b) we find *word-for-word* <note>original Latin: "verbatim"</note> the passages of the *Dagger of Faith* beginning *And Jacob went out* <note>original Hebrew: וייצא יעקב, "Vayeitzei Yaakov"</note> (folio 60¹), with the marginal readings, and that beginning *And it shall be in all* <note>original Hebrew: והיה בכל, "Ve-hayah bekhol"</note> (folio 280)². We have seen also³ that the history of *Bel and the Dragon* <note>an ancient story often associated with the Book of Daniel</note>, quoted partly by Martini from the *Genesis Rabbah*, is to be found word-for-word in our manuscript as a quotation from the *Great Midrash* <note>original: "Midrash Rabbah"</note> of Rabbah.

We have no means of contradicting all the charges of forgery which Abarbanel makes against Jerome of the Holy Faith <note>original: "Hieronymus de Sancta Fide"</note>, and implicitly against Martini. Their manuscripts, as we have already stated, are not at our command, and probably never will be. The destruction of Hebrew and Arabic manuscripts has been made in Spain wholesale. But *from one crime learn to know them all* <note>original Latin: "crimine ab uno disce omnes"; a classical phrase suggesting that one instance of wrongdoing reveals a person's general character</note>. The words *The oracle of the Lord to my Lord: Sit at my right hand* <note>original Hebrew: נאם ה' לאדוני שב לימיני, "Ne'um Hashem l'Adoni shev limini"; the opening of Psalm 110</note>, which Abarbanel says⁴ he did not find

# NOTE ON RAYMUND MARTINI’S DAGGER OF FAITH <note>original Latin: "Pugio Fidei"; a significant 13th-century anti-Jewish polemical work written by the Dominican friar Raymond Martini</note>.

in his copies, we read in the Manuscript Opp. 22, folio 66, which is a collection of <term>Midrashim</term> <note>ancient Jewish commentaries or interpretations of the Hebrew Bible</note> on the Psalms by Makhir ben Abba-Mari^1, as a quotation from the <term>Midrash Thillim</term> <note>a traditional commentary on the Book of Psalms</note>, where the passage runs word-for-word <note>original Latin: "verbatim"</note> as quoted by Martini^2. Fide," referring to the 15th-century convert and writer Gerónimo de Santa Fe</note>^3 from the <term>Mekhilta</term> <note>a collection of legal and homiletical interpretations on the Book of Exodus</note>, regarding which Abarbanel <note>Isaac Abarbanel, a famous 15th-century Jewish statesman and philosopher</note>^4 accuses him of having mixed up two <term>Agadic</term> <note>narrative or homiletic (non-legal) parts of Jewish literature</note> passages from two different <term>Midrashim</term>, is to be found word-for-word in the <term>Midrash Sh’moth Rabbah</term> <note>the "Great Exodus," a primary homiletic commentary on the Book of Exodus</note>^5. The confusion between <term>Mekhilta</term> and <term>Midrash Sh’moth Rabbah</term> can easily be explained, as both are <term>Agadic</term> commentaries on the Book of Exodus. The other so-called forgeries may turn out to be genuine when other manuscripts come to light in the future, or even from some <term>Midrashic</term> passages scattered through various printed books that have not been sufficiently noticed until now.

<meta>This page begins the English translation of the Aramaic text listed on page xxvii of the table of contents. It provides the opening of the Book of Tobit, contextualized by a rabbinic commentary on tithing.</meta>

# I. TRANSLATION OF THE ARAMAIC <note>original: "Chaldee"; this was the historical term used by 19th-century scholars to refer to the Aramaic language</note>.

## THE HISTORY OF TOBIAS <note>original: "Tobiyyah"; the Hebrew form of the name commonly known in English as Tobias</note>.

It is written in the <term>Midrash Rabbah</term> <note>a collection of ancient Jewish homiletic interpretations of the Torah</note>, in the section beginning ‘And Jacob went out’ (Genesis 28:10), in the seventieth section, on the passage ‘And of all that thou shalt give me I will surely give the tenth unto thee’ (Genesis 28:22). ‘Thou shalt truly tithe’ (Deuteronomy 14:22). Moses said to them, ‘Ye shall receive ten blessings if ye give the tenth.’ And so Jacob said, ‘And of all that thou shalt give me I will surely give the tenth unto thee.’ The ten blessings which thou shalt give me according as my father hath blessed me, on what merit will it be? On the merit of ‘I will surely give the tenth unto thee.’

### CHAPTER I.

The history is told of a pious man whose name was Tobi <note>likely a shortened form of Tobit or Tobias</note>, the son of Tobiel, of the tribe of Naphtali, who was led captive in the days of Shalmaneser, king of Assyria, and dwelt at Thisbe, a town of Naphtali, which is in Galilee. Now Tobi walked all his days in the right way, and he did many almsdeeds to his brethren and his nation who were with him in the captivity in Nineveh in the land of Assyria. And when he was but young in the land of Israel, all the tribe of Naphtali rebelled against the kingdom of David <note>referring to the political and religious split when the Northern Kingdom of Israel broke away from the Southern Kingdom of Judah</note>, and refused to go to Jerusalem. And they sacrificed to the calves which Jeroboam, king of Israel, had made at Bethel and Dan <note>these golden calves were established as alternative shrines to the Temple in Jerusalem</note>. And he alone went to Jerusalem at the times of the feasts, as it is written in the book of the law of Moses. And he brought thither the firstfruits and the heave-

<sig>c 2</sig>

<summary>This page introduces the Aramaic version of the Story of Tobias, prefaced by a rabbinic teaching on the importance of tithing (giving a tenth of one's income). It establishes Tobias as a righteous man of the tribe of Naphtali who remained faithful to Jerusalem despite the apostasy of the Northern Kingdom.</summary>
<keywords>Tobias, Tobit, Aramaic, Midrash Rabbah, Tithing, Naphtali, Shalmaneser, Assyria, Jeroboam, Golden Calves, Jerusalem</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Tobi (Tobit) during the Assyrian captivity, following his introduction as a pious man of the tribe of Naphtali. It details his adherence to Jewish law, his career in the Assyrian court, and his transition into a life of secret, dangerous charity under the reign of Sennacherib.</meta>

...offerings and the tithes, and gave them to the priests and Levites, to every one as it was meet to him, and ate the second tithe and the poor tithe <note>Tithing was a central theme in the introductory Midrash on the previous page; here, Tobi is shown putting these laws into practice even under difficult circumstances</note>, and gave according as everything is written in the book of Moses. And this Tobi was left an orphan by his father, and Deborah his father’s mother brought him up, and she led him in the true path. And when he became a man he took a wife of his own kindred, whose name was Hannah, and she bare him a son, and he called his name Tobiyyah <note>The Hebrew name meaning "The Lord is Good," commonly known in English as Tobias</note>. Now when Tobi was carried away captive he dwelt at Nineveh the great city. And all his brethren and kindred polluted themselves, and did eat the bread of the sons of the Gentiles <note>A reference to the dietary laws (kashrut) which forbade eating food prepared by non-Jews that might be ritually unclean or associated with idol worship</note>. But he ate not, because he feared God and loved him with all his heart. 

And therefore God gave him grace and favour in the eyes of Shalmaneser, king of Assyria, and he set him master over all that he had to the day of his death. And at that time he committed to the hand of Gabael, the brother of Kabri his kinsman, at the city Rages in the land of Media, ten talents of silver <note>A "talent" was a large unit of weight; ten talents of silver would have been a very significant fortune, equivalent to many years of wages</note>. And in the days of Tobi, Shalmaneser, king of Assyria, died, and Sennacherib his son reigned in his stead. And in those days the tribute became great, and Tobi could not go to the land of Media, for the travellers ceased by reason of the trouble, and he did not take the money from the hand of Gabael. 

And in the days of Sennacherib he did many almsdeeds to the poor, and he fed the hungry and the orphans, and clothed the naked, and performed many acts of kindness. And when he saw one slain, cast out in the street of the Jews, he buried him. Now when Sennacherib returned with confusion of face <note>original: "confusion of face"; a Hebrew idiom meaning he returned in shame and defeat, likely referring to the miraculous destruction of his army outside Jerusalem described in 2 Kings 19</note> from Judah, he went to Nineveh in fierce wrath against the ten tribes which were in the land of Assyria, and killed many of them, and their corpses were cast out in the street, and none buried them. When Tobi saw that, he was sore displeased therewith, and he rose in the night, and stole their corpses, and buried them. And thus he did many times. Once Sennacherib sought for the bodies of the slain, but found them not. And the men of Nineveh went and informed the king of Tobi that it was...

...he who had buried them. The king commanded that he should be put to death. When Tobi heard it, he arose and fled. And then the king commanded that they should spoil his house. But he hid from him for forty-five days, until **Adrammelech** and **Sharezer**, the king's sons, killed him with the sword, and they fled into the land of **Kardu** <note>Also known as the mountains of Ararat or Kurdistan</note>, and **Esarhaddon** his son reigned in his stead. And the king Esarhaddon appointed **Akikar** <note>The legendary sage Ahikar, portrayed here as Tobi's nephew</note>, the son of Hamael, his brother, over all his affairs, and he reigned over all the land of Assyria. And Akikar spake good words to the king for Tobi, and he begged of him, so that he brought him back to Nineveh, for Akikar was his friend and kinsman. And at that time they restored to him **Hannah** his wife and **Tobiyyah** his son.

### CHAPTER II.

Now it came to pass that, when the **feast of weeks** <term>Feast of Weeks</term> <note>Also known as Shavuot or Pentecost, a harvest festival celebrating the giving of the Torah</note> came, there was a plentiful meal prepared, and as he sat at the table he said to his son Tobiyyah, "Go and bring to me of our poor brethren, of such as fear God, to eat with us, and I tarry for thee until thou comest." 

Then Tobiyyah went and found a man slain, cast out in the street, and he told his father of it. When Tobi heard this, he rose from the table and did not eat, but went and lifted him up from the street of the city, and brought him into a house until the going down of the sun, that he might be able to bury him. And he returned to his house, and ate his bread with lamentation and mourning. And he said, "Woe that the prophecy of Amos is fulfilled in us, ‘And I will turn your feasts into mourning.’" And he wept very sore. 

And when the sun went down he went and buried him. But his kinsmen mocked him, saying, "This man feareth not for his soul, and he burieth the dead!" And on that night he did not wash himself <note>A reference to the ritual purification required after touching a corpse</note> clean of pollution from the dead, and he laid upon his bed by the side of the wall, and his face was uncovered, and he knew not that there were birds standing above him on the wall, and some of their dung fell upon his eyes, and a **whiteness** <note>Likely cataracts or a similar condition leading to blindness</note> came in his eyes. And every...

And he heard the kid crying in the house, and he asked her, "From whence hast thou this kid? it is perhaps stolen, render it to its owner, for it is not lawful for us to eat of anything that is stolen." She answered him, "It is not a stolen thing, but for the wages of the work of mine hands I received it." But Tobi did not believe the matter, and quarrelled with her concerning the kid. Hannah his wife answered and said to him, "Where are thy good deeds and thine alms? <term>alms</term> <note>Charitable gifts to the poor, which Tobi prioritized even above his own safety.</note> but thy reproach is manifest to all."

## CHAPTER III.

When Tobi heard this he was much grieved and did weep, and began to pray in the anguish of his soul, saying thus: "Thou art righteous, O great God, and all thy works are might, and all thy ways are goodness and truth, and thou art the judge of all the earth. Punish me not according to my sins and according to the sins of my fathers, for I and my fathers have sinned before thee, and we have transgressed thy commandments, and thou hast delivered us unto captivity and for a spoil and a reproach and a proverb to all the nations amongst whom thou hast exiled us. And now, O God, thy mercy is manifold and thy judgment is true, reward me not according to my sin, but deal with me according to thy great mercy, and take my soul out of mine hands, for it is better for me to die than to live in great misery and in this reproach, so shall I no more hear shame." 

And the same day **Sarah**, the daughter of **Reuel**, who lived at **Agbatanis** <note>Also known as Ecbatana, the capital of Media.</note>, a city in the land of Media, heard a great reproach, because she had been given to seven men to wife, and no man came in unto her according to the way of all the earth, for **Asmodeus**, king of the demons, killed them before they came in unto her according

...to the way of all the earth <note>original: "way of all the earth," a biblical idiom meaning to die.</note>. A maidservant said to her, "It is you who have killed your husbands! For you have been given to seven husbands, and not one of them came in unto you <note>A reference to the marriages not being consummated because the husbands were killed on their wedding nights.</note>, for you beat them. And for the husbands you have killed, may you die just as they have died, and may we never see a son or daughter from you for ever." 

And it came to pass, when Sarah heard these words, that she was very sorrowful and wept. And she went up to her father’s upper chamber, and wished to hang herself and so bring the old age of her father with sorrow unto the grave <note>A "lacuna," or missing section of the original manuscript, occurs here.</note>. <insert>And she said, I am the only daughter of my father;</insert> it is not good, therefore, for me to hang myself, but it is better for me to pray before God so that I shall hear no more reproach. 

At that time she spread out her hands in prayer before God, and said thus: "Blessed art thou, O Lord God, merciful and gracious, and blessed is thy holy name which is wonderful in all the world. Let all the works of thy hand bless thee for ever and ever. And now, O Lord, I lift my face before thee, and my eyes are fixed on thee. Bid me return to my dust <note>A poetic way of asking God to let her die.</note>, so that I may hear my reproach no more. It is manifest to thee, O Lord, that I am pure from all pollution with man, and that I have not polluted my name nor the name of my father in the land of my dwelling. I am the only daughter of my father, neither has he a son to inherit his property, nor has he a kinsman to whom he may leave me. And behold, seven husbands are dead for my sake, and what more is my life to me? But if it please thee not to kill me, have pity on me so that I hear reproach no more." 

On that day, the prayer of them both <note>Both Tobi in Nineveh and Sarah in Media.</note> went up before the throne of glory of the great God. And he sent the angel **Raphael** <note>His name means "God heals."</note> to heal them both—that is, to take away the <term>whiteness</term> <note>Likely cataracts or leukoma, described earlier as being caused by sparrow droppings.</note> from Tobi’s eyes, and to give Sarah, the daughter of Reuel, for a wife to **Tobiyyah**, the son of Tobi, and to take away **Asmodeus**, the king of the demons, from her. 

And when Tobi had finished his prayer, he returned to his house, and...

Sarah, the daughter of Reuel, came down from her father's upper chamber when she had made an end of praying.

### CHAPTER IV.

At that time Tobi <note>The Syriac form of the name Tobit.</note> remembered the money which he had committed to the hands of Gabael in the city Rages <note>An ancient city in Media, near modern-day Tehran, Iran.</note> in the land of Media. And he said within himself, "Behold, I have asked that my soul might die. I will call my son Tobiyyah <note>The Syriac form of the name Tobias.</note>, and will signify him of the matter of the money <note>Meaning he will inform his son about the deposit.</note> before I die." 

And he called his son Tobiyyah, and said to him, "My son, when I am dead, bury me with honour, and honour thy mother, and forsake her not all the days of her life, and do for her all that is right in her eyes, and oppose not the word of her mouth. Remember what pain she suffered for thee <note>A reference to the pains of childbirth and the labors of child-rearing.</note>, and when she is dead, bury her by me in one grave. 

And fear the Lord thy God all thy days, and let not thy will be set to sin, and transgress not the commandments. Do uprightly all thy days, and walk not with a violent man. For if thou deal truly it will be well with thee in all that thou possessest, and all who do uprightly—happy are they. 

My son, give alms <note>original: "zedaqah" — In many Semitic languages, the word for "justice" and "charity/alms" is the same.</note> of thy substance, and do not hide thee from a poor man, so God will not hide his Majesty from thee. My son, as far as it is in the power of thine hand to give alms, give, even if riches are far from thee. Give alms, and thou shalt acquire a good treasure for the day of wrath <note>A reference to the Day of Judgment.</note>, for it doth deliver from death, and suffereth not him that giveth it to descend into darkness: <insert>almsgiving</insert> is good, and whoso exerciseth it shall subsist by it. 

Is it not that our fathers were praised only for almsgiving? Of Abraham our father scripture declares, 'For I know him that he will command his children and his household after him . . . to do justice <note>The text notes that "justice" here is interpreted as "to give alms."</note> and judgment' (Gen. xviii. 19); of Isaac it is written, 'Then Isaac sowed in that land' <note>Gen. xxvi. 12; traditionally interpreted in this context as an act of charity or investment in the land's future.</note> (Gen. xxvi. 12); of Jacob it is written, 'And of all that thou shalt give me I will surely give the tenth unto thee' (Gen. xxviii. 22). My son, keep thyself from all counsel of sinners and from all whoredom, and take thee a wife of thine..."

<summary>Tobi, believing his death is near, gives his son Tobiyyah a series of moral exhortations, emphasizing the spiritual necessity of almsgiving and honoring one's parents. He also reveals the existence of a silver deposit left in Media.</summary>

<keywords>Tobit, Tobiyyah, almsgiving, charity, Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Rages, Media, moral instruction, burial rites, zedaqah</keywords>

->CH. V. TRANSLATION OF THE CHALDEE.<-

...own kindred, and take not a wife from the sons of the Gentiles <note>In this context, "Gentiles" refers to foreigners or those outside the covenant of Israel.</note>, for we are children of the prophets, for the first prophets were Noah, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, our fathers from the beginning. Remember that all these took wives from the seed of their brethren, and were blessed in their children, and their seed inherited the land. Now therefore, my son, love thy brethren, and let not thy heart be lifted up against the sons and daughters [of thy people], for in pride is much trouble, and it removeth from God <note>The original manuscript indicates these words were transposed (swapped in order).</note>. My son, give thy heart to all thy work, and what is hateful to thee do not thou to others <note>This is an early version of the "Golden Rule," appearing here in its negative formulation.</note>. Let not the wages of him that is hired abide with thee all night, and thy labour God will repay thee. Give of thy bread to the hungry, and of thy garments give to the naked, and let it not be hard in thine eyes <note>A Semitic idiom meaning "do not do it grudgingly" or with an unwilling heart.</note>. My son, spend freely <note>The original editor refers to a note on page xiv regarding the specific nuances of the word used for "spending freely" in this version.</note> thy bread and wine on the burial of the just, and hearken to good counsel at all times. Ask thy God, and he will direct thy paths, for there is no good counsellor to man but God, for whom he will he lifteth up, and whom he will he humbleth. My son, keep the words of my mouth and my commandments, and let them not depart from thine eyes. And now, my son, I signify to thee of the matter of my money, ten talents <term>Talent: A very large unit of weight for silver or gold; ten talents would represent a massive fortune, roughly 600–750 pounds of metal.</term> that I committed to the hand of Gabael at the city Rages in Media, for I know not the day of my death. Now therefore fear God, and keep thyself from all sin, and walk with him in humbleness, and he will give thee great riches.

## CHAPTER V.

Tobiyyah answered his father, "All that thou hast commanded me I will do; but how can I receive the money from the hand of Gabael, who knoweth me not, and I know not him? And what sign shall I give him so that he may believe me, and give me the money? And I know not the way that leadeth me to Media." Tobi answered and said to Tobiyyah, "My son, here is a sign for thee. His bag he gave me, and I gave him mine when I put the money in his hand to keep. And from that day..."

<summary>Tobi concludes his moral testament by advising Tobiyyah to marry within his tribe and live ethically, then reveals a deposit of ten talents held by Gabael in Media. Tobiyyah questions how he will claim the money, and Tobi explains they exchanged tokens—specifically bags—as a sign of the transaction.</summary>

...to this it is twenty years. And now, my son, seek thee a trusty man who may go with thee, and I will give him his wages. But he knew not that he was an angel of the Lord. He asked him, From whence art thou? He answered him, From the children of Israel, one of thy brethren. Tobiyyah went out and called him. And Raphael went to Tobi and said to him, Peace be to thee. Tobi said, Is it peace to me? why hath all this befallen me? for I see not the light of heaven, the sound of words I hear, but the man I see not, and I lie in darkness <note>Tobit had been blinded in a freak accident involving bird droppings while he slept in his courtyard.</note>. 

Raphael said, God is able to heal thine eyes, for thou art a pious man. Tobi said to him, My son Tobiyyah desireth to go to Media; canst thou go with him, and I will give thy wages? He said to him, I can; I am a messenger, and I know the ways, and the boundaries and the mountains are known to me. Tobi said, Tell me of what tribe thou art, and the name of the town where thou dwellest. Raphael said to him, If I am not right in thine eyes, go and seek another man who may go with thy son. Tobi said to him, My brother, be not provoked with me that I wish to know of a certainty thy name and of what...

# CHAPTER VI. TRANSLATION OF THE CHALDEE <note>The "Chaldee" refers to the Aramaic or Syriac version of the text from which this translation was derived.</note>

family thou art. He <note>Raphael, who is currently disguised as a man named Azaryah.</note> said to him, I am Azaryah, the son of Hananel, of the family of the great Salmiyyah, of thy brethren. Then Tobi said quietly and tranquilly, Come, my brother, be not angry with me because I have enquired to know thy family, for behold my brother is of a good family, and I know Hananel and Nathan, the two sons of the great Salmiyyah, as they went with me to Jerusalem when I dwelt in the land of Israel, and worshipped with me there, and these were not seduced at the time when our brethren erred <note>Tobi refers to the period when the Northern Tribes of Israel turned away from the Temple in Jerusalem to worship idols.</note>. Thou art of a good family, go in peace, and I will give thee thy wages, a drachm <note>A drachm was a silver coin, roughly equivalent to a day's wage for a laborer.</note> every day, and thy food the same as for my son, and I will add to thy wages <insert>if the Lord bring you back in peace</insert>. Raphael answered, Fear not, for I will go with thy son, and we shall return in peace. 

Tobi called his son Tobiyyah, saying to him, My son, prepare thyself, and go on the journey with thy brother; may the God of heaven lead you there in peace, and send his angel with you <note>There is dramatic irony here, as Tobi does not realize he is actually speaking to an angel.</note>, and prosper your journey, and bring you back in peace. Tobiyyah kissed his father and his mother, and they said to him, Go in peace. And they went out to go away. 

Then his mother wept, and said to Tobi, Wherefore is it thou hast not feared to send away the young man, for he is our only son, who goeth out and cometh in before us? <note>She means he is their sole companion and provider in their old age.</note> God hath kept us without the money <note>His mother suggests that their son's safety is more valuable than the debt they are seeking to collect.</note>. Tobi said to her, Be not afraid, he will go in peace, and he will return in peace, and the good angel will go with him, and his journey will be prosperous, and thine eyes shall see him return in peace. So make an end of weeping.

### CHAPTER VI.

The young man went and Raphael with him. And they came in the evening to the river Tigris, and they passed the night there. And Tobiyyah ran to the river to wash his feet, and a fish came suddenly out of the river, and devoured the young man's bread <note>In many versions of the Book of Tobit, the fish tries to swallow the boy's foot; in this specific Syriac version, it takes his food.</note>, and the young man cried out. Raphael said to him, Take the fish, and do not let it go. And he laid hold of the fish, and drew it to land. Raphael said to him,

**Azaryah**: The name assumed by the angel Raphael (meaning "God has helped").
**Hananel/Salmiyyah**: Ancestors recognized by Tobi, establishing "Azaryah's" credentials.
**Tigris**: One of the great rivers of Mesopotamia, which they must cross to reach Media.</note>

### HISTORY OF TOBIT. CH. VI.

Open the fish in the middle, and take out its heart, it is good to smoke thereof <note>This refers to the practice of fumigation—burning an object so its smoke can drive away spiritual or physical ailments.</note> before a man in whom the spirit of a demon and an evil spirit is, and they will flee from him; also the gall, to anoint therewith the eyes in which whiteness <note>This likely refers to cataracts or corneal scarring, which "blinded" Tobiyyah's father, Tobi, in the earlier chapters.</note> is, and they shall be healed. So Tobiyyah did, and took out the heart and the gall, and roasted the fish, and ate, and he left the remainder on the road. And they went to Media and came to Agbatanis <note>Historically known as Ecbatana, the capital of the Medes in modern-day Iran.</note>. Then Raphael said to Tobiyyah, My brother, thou comest to stay with Reuel, who is an old man, and hath a daughter who is exceeding fair, whose name is Sarah. And I will speak to him that he may give her to thee to wife. And she is the only child of her father, and he loveth her much. And she is a good woman and feareth heaven <note>A common idiom for being deeply devout and righteous.</note>. And when we return from Rages we will celebrate the marriage. For I know that Reuel will not oppose thy desire, and that he will give her to thee, and we shall bring her with us to thy father. 

Tobiyyah said to Raphael, I have heard that she hath been given to seven men, and they died before they came in unto her; and I have heard that Asmodeus <note>In Jewish and Near Eastern folklore, Asmodeus is a powerful king of demons often associated with destructive jealousy.</note>, the king of the demons, killed them. And now I fear the demon, lest peradventure he kill me, and I shall bring the old age of my parents with sorrow to the grave, and they have no other son nor daughter to bury them when they are dead. 

Raphael said to him, Dost thou not remember thy father’s precept which he gave thee, that thou shouldest marry a wife of the family of thy father? Now therefore hear me, and fear not the demon. I know that thou shalt take her to wife this night. And when thou shalt come into the marriage-chamber with her, take the heart of the fish, and smoke thereof under her garment. And the demon shall smell it, and he shall run away, and never come again. And when thou desirest to approach her, rise up both of you from the bed, and pray and ask for mercy from God, who hath commanded upon you his kindness, and who will give you healing. And thus thou shalt approach her, and beget from her children. Fear not, for she is appointed unto thee from the beginning, and thou shalt <insert>save her</insert>.

And **Tobiyyah** traveled on until he reached the city of **Akris** <note>Possibly a variation of the name of a city or landmark near the ancient capital of Assyria.</note>, which is opposite **Nineveh**. **Raphael** <note>The archangel traveling with Tobiyyah in the guise of a man.</note> said to **Tobiyyah**, "My brother, you know the condition in which you left your father. He told her everything that had happened. She was exceedingly glad and said to him, "Go now to your father, and I will stay here until your wife arrives."
