...prospered **Tobi** and **Tobiyyah** his son, and gave him children of his wife **Sarah**. And **Reuel** and **Ednah** his wife died, and **Tobiyyah** inherited all their goods.

After some days **Tobi** fell sick, and called his son **Tobiyyah**, and enjoined him the commandments of God, saying to him, "My son, do goodness all thy days to the poor and the rich, and give alms all thy days, for the sake of which God will bless all the works of thine hands. The Lord blessed **Abraham** our father on account of the alms and tithes <note>A religious contribution of one-tenth of one's income.</note> which he gave; and also **Isaac** for that he gave tithe and did almsdeeds; and so when **Jacob** went to the house of **Laban** and prayed, he vowed only to give tithe [and] alms to the poor, and therefore God made him prosperous, and gave him all that he asked, and preserved him from **Laban** and his brother **Esau**. And if thou do like as they did, he will bless thee as he blessed them." And he enjoined them other commandments, and when he had made an end of doing so, he was gathered to his people <note>An ancient idiom meaning he died and was buried with his ancestors.</note>. And **Tobiyyah** his son buried him with great honour. And after his death God blessed **Tobiyyah**, because he fulfilled the commandments of his father, and he made him exceeding prosperous, and bestowed blessing on all the works of his hands.

Behold we learn how great is the power of alms and tithes. Because **Tobi** gave alms and separated his tithes, as is meet, how the Holy One (blessed is he) rewarded him! And because the fathers of the world knew the power of alms, therefore they gave heed to them. Of **Abraham** it is written, ‘And he gave him tithes of all’ (Genesis 14:20); of **Isaac** it is written, ‘Then **Isaac** sowed in that land’ (Genesis 26:12), and ‘sowing’ means nothing else than alms, as it is said, ‘Sow to yourselves in alms’ <note>The original text likely cites Hosea 10:12, where the Hebrew word for "righteousness" (tzedakah) is interpreted in later Jewish tradition as "alms" or "charity."</note> (Hosea 10:12); of **Jacob** it is written, ‘And of all that thou shalt give me I will surely give the tenth unto thee’ (Genesis 28:22).

->PRAISE TO GOD!<-

<summary>This concluding page describes the death of Tobi and the subsequent prosperity of his son Tobiyyah, emphasizing the religious importance of charity (alms) and tithing. It draws parallels between Tobit's family and the biblical patriarchs Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob to illustrate God's rewards for generosity.</summary>

# II. TRANSLATION OF THE HEBREW.

## BOOK OF TOBIT.

### CHAPTER I.

This is the book of Tobi <note>Commonly known in English as Tobit; the name means "my goodness."</note>, the son of Tobiel, the son of Hananel, the son of Ariel, the son of Gabael, the son of Asael, the son of Nenathiel, of the tribe of Naphthali <note>One of the twelve tribes of Israel, traditionally located in the northern Galilee region.</note>, who was led captive from Samaria <note>The capital of the northern Kingdom of Israel.</note> with the captivity which was taken away in the days of Hoshea, the son of Elah, who was led captive in the days of Shalmaneser <note>Shalmaneser V, King of Assyria (reigned 727–722 BCE).</note>, king of Assyria. And he was of the inhabitants of a city of Naphthali, which is in Galilee, on the western boundary.

And Tobi said, Remember me, my God, for good, forasmuch as I have walked before thee all the days of my life in an upright way, and for the many almsdeeds <term>almsdeeds: acts of charity or giving money and food to the poor</term> and great kindnesses which I have done to my brethren and my nation in the captivity at Nineveh in the land of Assyria.

And she wept, and went up to her father’s upper chamber, <note>An "upper chamber" was a private room on the roof of a house, often used as a quiet place for prayer and reflection.</note> and cried before the Lord with a bitter voice, and said, O Lord God, thou hast given me to my parents, who are old and well stricken in age, and thou hast sent against my husbands that married me the king of the demons, <note>This demon is identified later as Asmodeus. Thou knowest, O Lord, that I am pure from all pollution with man, and that I have not polluted my name nor the name of my parents in the land of my captivity. And behold seven husbands are dead for my sake, and what profit have I in being any longer in the world? But if it be not good in thine eyes to kill me, look and answer me, and have mercy on me, that I may hear my reproach no more.

### CHAPTER V. TRANSLATION FROM THE HEBREW

...took from my hand mine <note>This likely refers to a "hand-bill" or a signed legal bond used as a receipt for the debt.</note>, when I put the money in his hand this day twenty years ago. So Tobiyyah went forth to seek a man to go with him to Media, and he found Raphael, an angel of the Lord, standing over against him, but Tobiyyah knew not that he was an angel of the Lord of hosts <note>original: "Adonai Tzevaot"; a title emphasizing God's command over the heavenly armies.</note>. Then the angel said to Tobiyyah, From whence art thou, young man? And Tobiyyah answered and said, I am of the children of Israel. Then Tobiyyah said, My lord, knowest thou how to go with me to Media? 

And the angel said, Yea, I know all the ways, and in Media I have been a guest in the house of our brother Gabael, who dwelleth at Rages, a city of Media, and it is a two days’ journey from Agbatanis <note>The city of Ecbatana, the ancient capital of Media.</note> to Rages, and Rages is built on a mountain, but Agbatanis is built on the plain. 

Then Tobiyyah said to him, Stay of thy kindness a moment, and I will go and declare the matter to my father, for I desire greatly that thou shouldest go with me, and I will give thee the wages of the journey. And he said to him, Go in haste, for behold I wait until thou comest back to me, and tarry not. 

Then Tobiyyah came and told his father, saying, I have found a good man of our brethren to go with me. And Tobi said, Call him to me, that I may know of what place he is, and whether he be trusty to go with thee. So Tobiyyah went forth and called him. And the angel came to Tobi, and said to him, Peace be unto thee, thou man of God. 

But Tobi said, If it is peace to me, why then hath all this befallen me, for I see not with mine eyes, but I sit blind in darkness? <note>Tobi responds with heavy irony, wondering how one can wish "peace" to a man suffering under the curse of blindness.</note> Then the angel said, He who hath deprived thee of light, the same shall heal thee, for thou art a righteous man. ### BOOK OF TOBIT. CHAPTER V.

“My son **Tobiyyah** is seeking to go to **Media** <note>An ancient region in what is now northwestern Iran.</note>. Can you go to Media? Can you go with him, and I will pay your wages?” And the angel said, “Yes, I can, for I know all the paths, and I have traveled through all the territories and know the mountains.” 

Then **Tobi** said, “From what place are you, from what tribe are you, and from what city are you?” 

And the angel said, “Are you still inquiring, when you have a hired man to go with your son according to your wish?” 

Then **Tobi** said, “My brother, I wish to know your name and what family you belong to.” 

And the angel said, “I am **Azaryah** <note>The name Azaryah means "The Lord has helped," a fitting name for an angel sent to provide assistance.</note>, the son of **Hananel**, from the family of the great **Shelomith**, of your own kinsmen.” 

Then **Tobi** said, “Life and peace to you! Now therefore, my brother, do not be offended with me because I inquired to know the history of your family’s ancestors. For behold, my brother, you are from a good and honorable family. You also know **Hananel** and **Nathan**, the two sons of the great **Shelomith**; they were the ones who went with me to **Jerusalem** when we lived in the land of **Israel** and worshipped with me there. They did not stray after the strange gods of the land like our other kinsmen. Now therefore, my brother, go in peace with my son, and may you both return in peace with the help of God. I will give you your wages: one <term>drachm</term> <note>A drachm was an ancient silver coin, roughly equivalent to a day's wage for a laborer.</note> every day, and your food just like my son’s. And if the **Holy One (blessed is he)** <note>original: "Ha-Kadosh Baruch Hu"; a common Jewish honorific for God.</note> brings you back in peace, I will add even more to your wages.” 

And the angel said, “Fear not, for I will go with your son. We shall go in peace, and we shall return in peace.” 

Then **Tobi** called his son and said to him, “Prepare what you need for the journey and go with your brother. May God Almighty lead you in peace, bring you back in peace, send his angel with you, and make your journey successful.” 

And **Tobiyyah** kissed his father and his mother, and they said to him, “Go in peace.” And they set out to go. 

Then his mother began to weep and said to her husband, “How could you not fear to send the young man away? He is the son of our old age, the one who goes out and comes in before us! Even without that money, our God would keep us alive.” 

And **Tobi** said to her, “Fear not, my sister, for he will go in peace and...”

### CHAPTER VI.

So the young man went on his way, and the angel **Raphael** <note>The name Raphael means "God heals." In this account, he is still disguised as the mortal Azaryah.</note> with him. And he came in the evening to the river **Tigris**, and they passed the night there. And **Tobiyyah** went down to the river to wash his feet. And a fish came suddenly out of the river, and devoured the young man’s bread. <note>In many versions of this story, the fish attacks Tobiyyah’s foot; here, it specifically targets his food.</note> And he cried out. Then the angel said to him, "Lay hold of the fish, and do not let it go." So the youth laid hold of the fish. 

Then the angel said to the young man, "Open the fish in the middle, and take the heart and the gall <note>The gall bladder, often used in ancient medicine to treat eye ailments.</note>, and put them by you, for they are good for healing." And the young man did so. And he cooked the fish, and ate, and the remainder he left. And they went on till they came to **Media**. 

Then **Tobiyyah** said to the angel, "My brother **Azaryah**, what healing will you perform with the heart and gall of the fish?" 

And he said to him, "The heart is good to smoke thereof <note>To burn as a medicinal or ritual incense.</note> before a man in whom is an evil spirit or the spirit of demons, and it will flee from him. And the gall is good for anointing the eyes in which is whiteness <note>Likely referring to cataracts or leukoma, a white opacity of the cornea.</note>, and they will be healed." 

And they came to **Agbatanis** <note>Also known as Ecbatana, the ancient capital of the Median Empire.</note>, and the angel said to **Tobiyyah**, "My brother, we shall pass the night in the house of **Reuel**, for he is an old man, and has an only daughter, fair of form, whose name is **Sarah**, and I will speak to him that he may give her to you as a wife. And she is of good understanding, and her father loves her. Now therefore hear me, and speak for her, and when we return from **Rages** <note>An ancient city near modern-day Tehran.</note>, we will celebrate the marriage. For I know that the man will not oppose your desire, and that he will not give her to a stranger, but you shall marry her, according to the **Law of Moses**, and we shall lead her to your father." 

Then **Tobiyyah** said to the angel, "I have heard, my brother, that she has already been given to..."

BOOK OF TOBIT. CH. VII.

...seven husbands, who died before they came in unto her, and I have heard that **Asmodeus** <note>In Jewish folklore, Asmodeus is a king of demons. Now therefore I am greatly afraid lest **Asmodeus** kill me, and I bring the old age of my parents in sorrow to the grave, for they have no other son, neither daughter, to bury them when they die. 

Then the angel said to him, "Fear the Lord, and remember him, and remember the commandments of thy father which he commanded thee, that thou shouldest take a wife of the family of thy father. Now therefore hear me, and be not afraid of the demon. For I know that thou shalt take her this night to wife. And when thou shalt enter the chamber with her, take the heart of the fish, and smoke thereof <note>To burn the organ as incense, creating a ritual smoke believed to drive away malevolent spirits.</note> under her garments, and the demon shall smell it, and flee, and return to her no more. And when thou shalt desire to approach her, rise ye from the bed, and pray, and supplicate the Lord that he would command his kindness and healing upon you, and heal her. And then thou shalt approach her, and shalt beget of her sons; and fear not, for for thee was she meet <note>Destined or prepared.</note> before the world was created, and by thine hand the Lord shall save her from the hand of the demon."

### CHAPTER VII.

And it came to pass when **Tobiyyah** heard all these sayings, that his soul was knit to the soul of **Sarah**. And they came to **Agbatanis** <note>original: "Agbatanis" — more commonly known as Ecbatana, the capital of Media, modern-day Hamadan in Iran.</note> to the house of **Reuel**, and found him by the door of his house, and they saluted him. And he saluted them again. And he said to them, "Go into the house in peace." And they went into the house. 

Then **Reuel** said to **Ednah** his wife, "How like is this youth to **Tobi** <note>The father, Tobit.</note> my brother!" 

And **Ednah** asked them, "My brethren, whence are ye?" 

And they answered her, "Of the captivity, which is in **Nineveh**, of the tribe of **Naphthali**." 

Then she said to them, "Know ye our brother **Tobi**?" 

And they said, "We know him." 

Then she said to them, "Is he well?" 

And they said, "He is well." 

Then **Tobiyyah** said, "Your brother **Tobi**, of whom ye speak, is my father." 

And **Reuel** ran to meet him, and embraced him, and kissed him, and wept with him.

## CH. VIII. TRANSLATION OF THE HEBREW.

And Reuel said, "Blessed art thou, my son, of the Lord, for thou art the son of a righteous and upright man." And Reuel and Ednah his wife and Sarah his daughter wept yet more with him. My son, know that I have already given her to seven husbands <note>The number seven often symbolizes a complete or ultimate trial; here it underscores the lethal power of the demon Asmodeus who claimed the lives of all previous suitors.</note>, who all died before they came in unto her. But **Tobiyyah** said, "I will neither eat nor drink before thou hast given her to me to wife." Then Reuel said, "Take her, for she is thy sister, and thou art her brother <note>In this historical context, "sister" and "brother" are terms of endearment and kinship, signifying they belong to the same tribe and are therefore legally and spiritually suited for one another.</note>. Behold, I give her to thee to wife, according to the law of **Moses** and **Israel**, and may the Lord God of heaven make you dwell this night in peace, and command upon you his kindness and his peace." 

Then Reuel took **Sarah** his daughter, and gave her to **Tobiyyah** to wife, and he blessed them, and bade Ednah his wife bring him a tablet, and he wrote thereon the deed of marriage <note>A formal legal document, similar to the Jewish 'Ketubah,' which established the husband's obligations and the wife's protections.</note>, and he sealed it before witnesses. And they ate and drank and were merry. And Reuel said to Ednah his wife, "Prepare the chamber, and put them therein." And Ednah embraced **Sarah** her daughter, and wept with her, and said to her, "My daughter, may the Lord God of **Israel** shew thee kindness this night, and grant thee mercy, and have pity on thee because of the sorrow which hath passed over thee unto this day."

### CHAPTER XIII.

At that time **Tobiyyah** wrote down all these things with joy. And **Tobi** said,

> Blessed is the Lord the great God, who doeth marvellous things to his people and his servants. He smiteth and healeth, and killeth and maketh alive, and bringeth down to hell <note>In this context, "hell" refers to *Sheol*, the place of the dead, rather than a place of punishment.</note> and lifteth up. Who hath dispersed us among the **Gentiles**; we are bound to publish all these marvellous works among the nations. And ye, O children of **Israel**, be strong, and let your heart be of good courage, and let not your hands be weak, for your work shall be rewarded, and he will wait that he may be gracious unto you, and will be exalted that he may have mercy upon you. For the Lord is a God of judgment; blessed are all they that wait for him. And ye, my children, continue in alms <term>alms: charitable donations or acts of mercy given to the poor as a religious duty</term>, prayer, and supplication before the lord of all the world, for alms and prayer drive back the decree <note>The "decree" refers to a divine judgment or a period of suffering destined for a person or nation.</note>, for it is said, ‘And alms do deliver from death.’ And blessed is the Lord, who hath shewn to me and my father and my forefathers, and every one who hath trusted in him, wonders, and marvels, and great and terrible things. O lord of the world, shew us in our days salvation and redemption by the coming of the **Redeemer** <term>Redeemer: A reference to the expected Messiah who would deliver Israel from exile</term> and the building of **Ariel** <note>Ariel is a symbolic name for Jerusalem, often interpreted as "Lion of God" or referring to the Temple altar.</note> before the eyes of all **Israel**, as it is said, ‘In his days **Judah** shall be saved and **Israel** shall dwell safely;’ and it is written, ‘And the ransomed of the Lord shall return;’ and again, ‘The Lord doth build up **Jerusalem**, he gathereth together the outcasts of **Israel**.’ Blessed is the Lord for ever, Amen and Amen.

## III. ADDITION TO THE MIDRASH TANḤUMÂ <term>Midrash Tanḥumâ: a collection of ancient Jewish homilies and teachings organized according to the portions of the Torah</term>.

->Section beginning ‘Give ear’ (Deuteronomy 32:1).<-

AND thus it is found in the book of Rabbi Moses the Preacher <note>original: "R. Moses had-Darshan," an 11th-century scholar from Narbonne known for compiling Midrashic materials</note>. ‘He kept him as the apple of his eye’ (Deuteronomy 32:10). The Holy One (blessed is he) keeps those whom he tries like a man who keeps the apple <note>In ancient usage, the "apple" refers to the pupil of the eye, the most sensitive and protected part.</note> of his eye. We have an example in the following history. 

There was a certain man, rich, of high station, and learned in the oral law <term>oral law: the traditional interpretations of the Torah, such as the Mishnah and Talmud, which were originally passed down by word of mouth</term>; the same had a daughter, of exceeding beauty, and moreover pious, who had been married three times to three men, but each time on the morning after the first night of her marriage they found her husband dead. She said, "Men shall die for me no more, I will dwell in widowhood and seclusion, until God shall look on me, and take compassion." 

And so she remained many days. Now that rich man had in another city a very poor brother, who had ten sons; and every day he and his eldest son brought in bundles of sticks from the wood, and sold them, and by this means he and his wife and children supported themselves. Once they did not sell any, and they had no money to buy bread, and that day they ate nothing. 

On the morrow it came to pass, when they went into the wood, that the father fainted away. The son’s eyes ran with tears because of their poverty, and he lifted his eyes to heaven. The son considered in his heart, and, having taken leave of his father and mother, went to the city where his uncle dwelt. And when he entered his house, his uncle and likewise his wife and daughter were exceedingly

### ADDITION TO THE MIDRASH TANḤUMÂ <term>Midrash Tanḥumâ: a collection of ancient Jewish teachings and stories that explain the meanings of the Torah</term>

He was glad and asked about his father, his mother, and his children. He stayed with him for seven days, and at the end of those seven days, the young man went to his uncle and said, "I have one request to ask of you; do not refuse me." His uncle said to him, "Tell me, my son, what is it that you desire?" He replied, "Swear an oath to me." And so the uncle did. 

Then the young man said, "This is the request I ask of you: give me your daughter to be my wife." 

When the man heard this, he wept. "No, my son," he said, "No—for such is her way, unfortunately." <note>This is a delicate way of referring to the fact that her previous three husbands died on their wedding nights.</note> 

The young man replied, "I ask even on these terms." 

The uncle said to him, "If you are eager for her for the sake of my riches, do not marry her. I will give you silver and gold in abundance, for you are a handsome and wise young man. Take my advice: do not put yourself in danger with her." 

He answered, "You have already sworn an oath concerning this matter." 

The rich man saw how the matter stood and consented. He went to his daughter and told her what had passed between them. When she heard this, she wept and cried out in the bitterness of her soul. She lifted her eyes to heaven and said, "Lord of the worlds, let your hand be upon me, but let not all these men die for my sake!" 

What did the uncle do? He betrothed <term>betrothed: the formal legal stage of a Jewish marriage that precedes the wedding ceremony</term> her, prepared a banquet, invited the elders of the city, and made a canopy <term>canopy: the wedding chuppah, a cloth structure under which the marriage ceremony takes place</term>, and the bridegroom sat inside it. 

And a certain elder met him—it was **Elijah of blessed memory** <note>Elijah is a prophet in Jewish tradition who is believed to return to Earth in disguise to help those in need or to offer divine wisdom.</note>—and he called the young man aside privately and said to him: "My son, I will give you right counsel; do not depart from my advice. When you sit down to eat, a poor man will come in to you. He will be dressed in black and tattered garments, he will be barefoot, and his hair will be standing up like nails <note>original: "Job 4:15." This vivid description of hair standing on end emphasizes the poor man's frightening or supernatural appearance.</note>. He is so poor that there is none like him in all the world. When you see him, you must rise from your seat and seat him beside you. Make him eat and drink; wait on him with all your ability and pay him honor. Let not a single word of what I have said to you fall to the ground, and so you shall [be saved]..."

IV. THE OLD LATIN BIBLE. THE BOOK OF TOBIT. <term>Itala: refers to the "Old Latin" versions of the Bible used by the early Church before St. Jerome's Vulgate became the standard Latin translation.</term>

### CHAPTER I.

¹ The book of the words of Tobit, son of Tobiel, son of Hananiel, son of Gabahel, son of Asiel, son of Gadalel, son of Arabei, of the tribe of Naphtali; ² who was taken away as a captive in the days of Shalmaneser, king of the Assyrians, from the city of Bihel, which is on the right side of Edisse, a city of Naphtali, in the upper parts of Galilee above Naasson, behind the road which leads to the west, on the left side of Raphain. I, Tobit, walked in the way of truth and justice all the days of my life: ³ and I gave many alms <note>original: "eleemosynas" — charitable gifts or acts of mercy, a central theme in the Book of Tobit</note> to my brothers, and to my nation, and to all who were with me in captivity, in the land of the Assyrians in Nineveh. ⁴ And when I was in my own land of Israel, being the youngest among all, the entire tribe of Naphtali my father departed from the house of David and from the city of Jerusalem—which was chosen from all the tribes of Israel, where the altar was established for all the tribes of Israel, and which was sanctified for all ages. At that time, when the temple of the dwelling of the Most High God had been built in Jerusalem, so that every generation might sacrifice in it forever, ⁵ both all my brothers and the entire house of Naphtali my father would sacrifice at Dan to the golden calf <note>A reference to the northern kingdom's idolatry; King Jeroboam set up golden calves in Dan and Bethel to keep the people from traveling to Jerusalem to worship</note>, which Jeroboam the king of Israel had made, and upon all the high mountains of Galilee. ⁶ But I alone went many times to Jerusalem on the feast days, just as it is written for all Israel in an everlasting decree: bringing with me the firstfruits and the tithes of the herds and the flocks, and the first shearings of my sheep, I gave these to the priests, the sons of Aaron, as was the custom, along with the wheat, wine, oil, figs, pomegranates, and other fruits.

<summary>This page introduces the lineage and character of Tobit, a righteous Israelite of the tribe of Naphtali living in Assyrian captivity who remained faithful to Jerusalem's laws while his kinsmen turned to idolatry.</summary>

# CH. I. 23. ITALA.

I distributed [tithes] to the Levites and those serving the Lord who were present in Jerusalem; and the second tithe I converted into money every six years, and I would go and spend it in Jerusalem in the holy place each year. ^7 And the third tithe I brought to the converts <note>original: "proselytis" — people who had joined the Jewish faith</note>, the orphans, and the widows, doing all that was commanded in Israel; and I gave it to them in the third year. ^8 And I ate it according to the commandment which was written concerning them in the Law of Moses, and just as Deborah—the mother of my father Tobiel—had commanded, for my father had left me an orphan when he died.

^9 And after I became a young man, I took a wife named Anna from my own nation, and I fathered a son by her and named him Tobias. ^11 And after I was taken into captivity to the Assyrians in Nineveh, ^12 and all my brothers who were of my kin ate the bread of the Gentiles <note>The "bread of the Gentiles" refers to food that did not conform to Jewish dietary laws (kosher), which was a significant test of faith for Jews living in exile</note>; I, however, guarded my soul so that I would not eat of their food. ^13 And because I was mindful of God with all my heart, the Most High Lord granted me favor in the presence of Shalmaneser <note>original: "Salmannassar" — Shalmaneser V, the Assyrian king who conquered the Kingdom of Israel</note>, king of the Assyrians, ^14 and I used to purchase for him everything he wanted for his use, ^16 traveling into the region of Media until he died.

^17 And I entrusted to Gabelus, my kinsman <note>original: "fratri" — literally "brother," but often used for relatives or fellow countrymen</note> the son of Gabahel, in the city of Rages in the region of the Medes, ten talents of silver <term>talent: a massive unit of weight for currency; ten talents was an enormous sum, roughly equivalent to 600–750 pounds of silver</term>. ^18 And after Shalmaneser the king of the Assyrians died, his son Sennacherib <note>original: "Sennacherim" — the king famous for his unsuccessful siege of Jerusalem during the time of King Hezekiah</note> reigned in his place; the roads into the land of the Medes became unstable, and no one could travel there anymore.

^19 And in the days of King Shalmaneser, I performed many acts of charity <note>original: "eleemosynas" — charitable gifts or deeds of mercy</note> for all those of my nation, ^20 sharing my bread with the hungry and clothing the naked; and if I saw any of my nation who had died and been thrown behind the wall of Nineveh, I buried them. ^21 I also buried those whom King Sennacherib had killed when he fled from Judea because of the judgment the Lord of Heaven executed upon him for his blasphemies; for in his anger he killed many of the children of Israel. I would wrap their bodies and bury them; and the king searched for the bodies but could not find them. ^22 And it was reported to him that I was burying them, and he sought to kill me. ^23 I fled, and all my property was seized; nothing was left to me except my wife Anna and my son Tobias.

They gave her her full wages, ²⁰ and in addition to her payment for the finished work, they gave her a young goat for food. ²¹ When the goat came into the house with me, it began to bleat. I called my wife and said to her, "Where is this bleating goat from? See to it that it was not stolen! Return it to its owners, for it is not lawful for us to eat anything that is stolen." But she replied and said to me, "It was given to me as a gift in addition to my wages." I did not believe her, but continued to tell her, "It is stolen; return it to its owners." I argued with her and felt humiliated in her presence because of this matter. ²² Then she became angry because I did not believe her, and responding, she said to me, "Where are your acts of righteousness? <note>Anna is mockingly questioning the value of Tobit's famous charity and piety</note> ²³ Look, what you are suffering is known to everyone!"

### CHAPTER III.

¹ Sorrowful in spirit, I groaned and wept. I went into my courtyard and began to pray with the groaning of my soul, ² and I said: "You are righteous, O Lord, and all Your works are great; all Your ways are full of mercy and truth, and You judge with true judgment forever. ³ And now, O Lord, remember me and look upon me. Do not take vengeance for my sins, nor for my ignorance, nor for the sins of my parents who sinned before You. ⁴ For they did not obey Your commandments, and You delivered us over to plundering, captivity, and death—to be a cautionary tale, a proverb, and a disgrace among all the nations where You have scattered us."

<summary>Tobit describes how he was accidentally blinded by sparrow droppings and the subsequent poverty that forced his wife, Anna, to work. A dispute over a goat leads Tobit into a deep depression and a prayer of lamentation for his and his ancestors' sins.</summary>

5 And now, Lord, your many judgments are true, which you exact from me for my sins and those of my parents; for we have not acted according to your precepts, nor have we walked sincerely before you. 6 And now, Lord, do with me according to what pleases you, and command my spirit to be taken from me, so that I may now be released from the earth. For it is better for me to die than to live, because I hear false reproaches <note>original: "improperia" — insults or shaming remarks that damage one's social standing</note>, and I am in great sorrow. Therefore, Lord, command that I be released from this necessity, and give me rest in the eternal place. Do not turn your face away from me, for it is better for me to die than to live and to suffer such hardship in my life, so that I may no longer hear the insults of men.

7 On that same day, it happened that Sarah, daughter of Raguel, who was in Ecbatana <note>The capital of Media, located in modern-day Iran</note>, a city of the Medes, also heard insults from one of her father’s maidservants. 8 This was because Sarah had already been given in marriage to seven husbands, and Asmodeus <note>original: "Asmodaeus" — a powerful demon of destruction or lust mentioned in ancient Near Eastern and Jewish lore</note>, a most wicked demon, killed them at the very hour they went in to be with her, as is the custom for wives.

9 And her maidservant said to her:
> It is you who strangles your husbands! Look, you have already been given to seven men, and you have enjoyed none of them. Why do you lash out at us? Is it because of your husbands who have died? Go and join them, and may we never see a son or daughter from you forever!

10 In that same hour, the girl’s soul was grieved, and weeping, she went up to the upper room of her father’s house. She intended to end her life with a noose <note>original: "laqueo" — she considered hanging herself</note>, but she thought to herself:
> Perhaps they will reproach my father and say, "You had only one beloved daughter, and she hanged herself." Thus I would begin to bring down my father’s old age with sorrow of heart to the world below <note>original: "ad inferos" — a reference to Sheol, the dwelling place of the dead</note>. It is not useful to flee life with a noose;

11 but it is better instead to pray to the Lord that I might die, so that I may no longer hear insults in my life, neither I nor my father.

12 At that same time, stretching out her hands toward the window, she prayed, 13 and said:
> Blessed are you, O Lord God of mercies, and blessed is your holy and honorable name throughout all ages. May all your works bless you forever.
>
> 14 And now, Lord, I turn my face to you, and I direct my eyes toward you. 15 Command that I now be released from the earth, so that I may not hear the insults of men.

[I am free] from all uncleanness with man, and I have not defiled my body, nor have I disgraced my father’s name in the land of my captivity. For her inheritance was destined for Tobias above all others who had desired her. At that same moment, Tobit returned from the courtyard into his house, and Sarah, the daughter of Raguel, also came down from her upper room.

## CHAPTER IV.

1 On that same day, Tobit remembered that he had entrusted money to Gabelus in Rages, a city of the Medes. He thought in his heart: "Behold, I have asked for death; why do I not call my son Tobias and tell him about this money which I entrusted to Gabelus before I die?" So he called his son 2 and said to him: "Son." And he answered: "What is it, father?" And Tobit said: 3 "Son, when I have died, bury me properly. Honor your mother and do not abandon her all the days of her life; do what is pleasing to her in her sight, and do not grieve her spirit in any matter. 4 Remember, son, how many dangers she suffered for you while you were in her womb. 5 And when she has died, bury her beside me in the same tomb. 6 All the days of your life, son, keep God in your mind, and do not desire to sin or to set aside His commandments. Do what is right all the days of your life, and do not walk in the way of injustice; for if you act according to the truth, there will be success in your works and for all who practice righteousness. 7 From your sub— <note>The text breaks off here at the start of the word "substantia" (substance/wealth).</note>

[...from your] substance, my son, give alms <note>original: "eleemosyna" — From the Greek *eleemosyne*, meaning mercy or charity. In this context, it refers to giving to those in need.</note>, and do not turn your face away from any poor person: and so it shall be that the face of God will not be turned away from you.
8 However much you have, my son, give alms accordingly. 9 If you have an abundance of wealth, give more from it as alms; if you have little, share even from that small amount; and do not be afraid, my son, when you give alms. 10 You will be storing up a good reward for yourself for the day of necessity: 11 for almsgiving delivers from death, and does not allow one to go into the darkness. 12 Almsgiving is a good gift for all who perform it in the presence of the Most High God. 13 Watch yourself, my son, against all immorality <note>original: "fornicatione" — Here meaning sexual immorality or straying from the religious and social laws of the community.</note>: take a wife from the lineage of your parents, and do not take a foreign woman, who is not from the tribe of your parents: for we are children of the prophets, who truly prophesied of old. Noah prophesied first, and Abraham, and Isaac, and Jacob, our parents from the beginning of the world. Remember, my son, that all these took wives from the race of their fathers, and were blessed in their children, and the seed of their children shall possess the inheritance of the earth. 14 And you, my son, love your brothers; and do not act with a proud heart toward the daughters of the people of your own nation, so as not to refuse to take one of them as a wife: for in pride there is destruction and great instability; and in extravagance <note>original: "luxuria" — Refers to excess, indulgence, or even spiritual apathy.</note> there is loss and great impiety. 15 Pay the wages of any man who has worked for you on the same day, and do not let a man’s wages remain with you: and your reward will not be diminished if you serve God in truth. 16 Watch yourself, my son, in all your works, and be wise in all your words: and what you hate, do not do to another. <note>original: "quod oderis, alio ne feceris" — This is an early formulation of the 'Golden Rule,' expressed here in the negative: "Do not do to others what you would not want done to yourself."</note> Do not drink wine to the point of drunkenness, and let no wickedness accompany you in all your life. 17 Share your bread with the hungry, and cover the naked with your clothes. From all things that you have in abundance, my son, give alms; and let your eye not be begrudging when you give alms. 18 Pour out your wine and your bread upon the graves of the righteous <note>original: "super sepulcra justorum" — An ancient practice of offering food and drink at tombs, often to be gathered by the poor in honor of the deceased.</note>, and do not give it to sinners. 19 Seek counsel from a wise person, and do not despise it; for every piece of counsel is useful. 20 At all times bless God: and ask of him that your ways may be directed, and all your paths and thoughts well-disposed...

## Chapter V.

1 Then Tobias answered Tobit his father, saying: All things whatever you have commanded me, father, I will do. 2 But how will I be able to receive this money from him? For he does not know me, nor I him: what sign will I give him, so that he knows me, and believes, and gives me this money? Also, I do not know the roads of that region. 3 And Tobit answered his son, saying: He gave me his handwritten note <note>original: "Chirographum" — a legal document or promissory note written in the debtor's own hand.</note>, and likewise he received mine, and it was divided into two parts: I took one, and the other I placed with the money itself; and it is now twenty years since I deposited this money with him. 4 Now then, son, look for some faithful man who may go with you for a wage: and while I am still alive, receive the money from him. 5 And Tobias went out to look for a man who would lead him into the region of Media, and who especially had knowledge of the road of that region: and he found the angel Raphael standing. 6 And not knowing that he was an angel of God, he said to him: Where are you from, young man? 7 And he answered, saying: I have come here from the children of Israel, your brothers, to work. And Tobias said to him: Do you know the road that leads to the region of Media? 8 And he said: I know many things: and I am familiar with all the roads, and several times I have gone into that region; and I stayed with Gabelus our brother, who lives in Rages, a city of the Medes: and it is a two-day journey from Bathanis to Rages, the city of Phagar, which is situated on the mountain; and Bathanu is in the middle of the plain. 9 And Tobias said to him: Wait, young man, until I go in and announce this very thing to my father.

...for it is necessary that you go with me, and I will give you your wages. And the angel Raphael, answering, said: Behold, I am waiting for you; do not delay. 

10 And Tobias went in and told his father Tobit, saying: Behold, I have found a man from among our own people <note>original: "ex fratribus nostris" — literally "from our brothers," a common way of referring to fellow Israelites.</note> who will go with me. And Tobit said: Call the man to me, my son, so that I may know from what tribe he is, and whether he is a faithful companion to whom you may be safely entrusted. 

And Tobias went out and called him, saying: Young man, come in; my father asks for you. 

11 And when he had entered, Tobit greeted him first. And the angel said: May joy be with you always, brother. 

12 And Tobit answered and said: What joy can there be for me? I am a man weak in my eyes, and I do not see the light of heaven, but I am placed in darkness, like the dead among the living. I hear the voices of men, but I do not see them. 

13 And the angel Raphael said to him: Be of strong heart; it is near at hand that you shall be healed by God. 

14 And Tobit answered him: My son Tobias wishes to go into the region of Media; if you can go with him and lead him, I will give you your wages, brother. 

15 And the angel Raphael said: I will be able to go with him, for I know all the roads, and I have traveled several times into the region of Media; I have crossed all its plains and mountains, and I know all its routes well. 

16 And Tobit said: Brother, from what family and from what tribe are you? Tell me. 

17 And he said: Is it necessary for you to know my family or my tribe? You are looking for a hired worker; why do you ask about my lineage? 

18 But if you insist on knowing, I am Azarias, son of the great Ananias, from among your brothers. 

19 And Tobit said to him: May you come safe and sound, brother. But I ask that you be not angry that I wished to truly know your family. For you are from among my brothers, of a good and noble lineage. Do you know Ananias and Nathan, the two sons of the great Shemaiah <note>original: "Semeiae" — the Latin form of the Hebrew name Shemaiah.</note>, who also went with me to Jerusalem and worshipped there with me, and did not go astray? These are all our brothers, and they are excellent men. You are from a good root, brother; may you go safely and come back safely. 

And he added, saying: I will give you, as wages, a silver drachma <term>didragmam: a silver coin or "didrachm." In this context, it represents a standard daily wage for a laborer.</term> per day, and provide whatever things are necessary for you, and for my son likewise. Go with him, and I will add even more to your wages. 

20 And the angel Raphael said to him: I will go with him, do not fear.

### CHAPTER VI.

1 And their son set out, and the angel with him, and a dog followed them <note>The mention of the dog is a famous and unique detail in the Book of Tobit, often highlighted in Christian art to symbolize fidelity.</note>: and they went along together, and the first night overtook them, and they stayed by the river Tigris. 2 And Tobias went down to wash his feet in the river, and a large fish leaped out of the water and tried to swallow the boy's foot <note>original: "pene puerum devoraverat" — literally "it almost devoured the boy." Historical commentators have debated if this was a large catfish or even a crocodile.</note>. 3 And the boy cried out. 4 And the angel said to him: Catch him and hold him. And the boy caught the fish and drew it onto the land. 5 And the angel said to the boy: Gut this fish, and take its gall, heart, and liver, and put them away and keep them with you: for these are necessary for useful medicines <note>The use of animal organs for medicinal or apotropaic (evil-averting) purposes was a common practice in the ancient world.</note>; and throw away the other entrails. 6 And the boy gutted the fish, and took the gall, heart, and liver: and they roasted part of the fish and took it on the way; the rest they salted: and they began their journey until they reached the region of the Medes. 7 And the boy questioned the angel, saying: Brother Azarias, what remedy is there in this gall, heart, and liver of the fish? 8 And the angel said: The heart and the liver are to be smoked before a man or a woman...

and he said to him: "Eat and drink, and may it be pleasant for you this night; for there is no other to whom it is proper to receive Sarah my daughter than you; likewise, it is not permitted for me to give her to any other man than you: you are my closest kinsman, and Sarah belongs to you. ¹¹ But I will tell you the truth, my son: I have already given her to seven men, our brothers; and they all died when they went in to her <note>original: "secedunt" - literally 'withdrew' with her to the bedchamber.</note>. Now therefore, my son, eat and drink." And Tobias said: "I will not eat anything here, nor drink, until you confirm the matter with me." ¹³ And Raguel said: "Do not doubt, my son, I will do what you wish." And having said these things, he added, saying: ¹⁴ "Sarah is destined for you according to the judgment of the Book of Moses <note>Referring to the laws of inheritance and marriage within the tribe found in the Torah.</note>, and it has been judged from heaven that she should be given to you. Receive your sister <note>A term of endearment for a kinswoman of the same tribe; they are cousins.</note>; from now on you are her brother, and she is your sister: she is given to you from this day and forever. And may the Lord of heaven arrange this night well for you, and show you mercy and peace."

¹⁵ And Raguel called Sarah his daughter, and she came to him: and, taking the young woman's hand, he handed her over to him, and said: "Look, receive her according to the law and the judgment that is written in the Law of Moses, to give this woman to you as a wife. Have her therefore, and lead her to your father, my son, safe and sound: and may the God of heaven give you a good journey, and peace." ¹⁶ And he called the mother and the girl, and ordered paper <note>original: "chartam" - likely a piece of papyrus or parchment for a legal contract.</note> to be brought, so that he might make a marriage contract <term>conscriptionem conjugii: a formal written agreement or 'ketubah' documenting the marriage</term>, just as he had given her as a wife according to the judgment of the Book of Moses. And her mother brought the paper, and he wrote, and signed it. ¹⁷ And from that hour they began to eat and drink.

¹⁸ And Raguel called Anna his wife, and said to her: "Prepare another bedchamber, and lead the girl there." And she went into the bedchamber, and prepared it, as she was told. ¹⁹ And she led her daughter in, and she wept for her sake: and she wiped away the tears, ²⁰ and said to her: "Be of strong courage, daughter: may the Lord of heaven give you joy in place of your sorrow <note>original: "taedio" - weariness or grief from her long-standing affliction.</note>." And she went out from there.

### CHAPTER IX.

$^{1}$ Then Tobias called for the angel Raphael, and said to him: $^{3}$ "Brother Azarias, <note>Azarias is the human name the angel Raphael used to disguise his identity while traveling with Tobias.</note> take with you four servants and two camels from here, and travel to the city of Rages to Gabael: and give him his promissory note <note>original: "chirographum" — a handwritten legal bond or receipt for the money Tobit had left in Gabael's care years earlier.</note>, and receive the money, and invite him to the wedding. $^{4}$ For you know that my father is counting the days: and if I delay even one day more, I will grieve his soul. $^{5}$ But you see how Raguel has sworn an oath, and we cannot disregard his oath." $^{6}$ And Raphael the angel went with four servants and two camels to the city of Rages: and they stayed with Gabael, and Raphael gave him his promissory note. $^{7}$ And he informed him about Tobias, the son of Tobit, that he had taken a wife, the daughter of Raguel, and was inviting him to the wedding. And immediately Gabael arose and produced the bags with their seals <note>The money had been kept under seal for twenty years to ensure it remained untampered with.</note>, and they loaded them onto the camels; and they kept watch together, and came to the wedding. $^{8}$ And Gabael found Tobias reclining at the table: and he leaped up and greeted him, and kissed him: and Gabael wept and blessed God, $^{9}$ saying: "Blessed be the Lord who has given you peace, good and excellent man, for you are the son of a good, excellent, and just man who performs acts of charity <note>original: "eleemosynas" — the practice of almsgiving, which is a central virtue throughout the Book of Tobit.</note>: and blessed are you, my son. $^{10}$ May the Lord of heaven give a blessing to you and to your wife, and to your wife’s father and mother: and blessed be God, for I see the likeness of my cousin <note>original: "consubrini" — a kinsman or close relative.</note> Tobit."

### CHAPTER X

¹ And every day Tobit was counting the days required for his son to go and return. And after the days were completed, and his son did not come, he said: "Has Tobias perhaps been detained? ² Or perhaps Gabael is dead, and there is no one to return the money to him?" ³ And he began to be heavy of heart; and Anna his wife said: "My son has perished, and is no longer among the living; why is he so late?" ⁴ And she began to weep, and to mourn for her son, saying: "Woe is me, my son, that I let you go, the light of my eyes!" ⁶ To her Tobit said: "Be silent, do not be heavy of heart, sister <note>original: "soror" — as seen previously, Tobit uses this as an affectionate term for his wife.</note>, our son is safe; but perhaps some delay detains them; for the man who went with him is faithful, and one of our kinsmen. Do not be distressed for him, sister; he is already coming." ⁷ But she said: "Be silent, you are annoying me; do not deceive me, my son has perished." And springing up, she would look around the road by which her son had set out, and she ate nothing; and when the sun had set, she would go inside, and mourn weeping all night, and she did not sleep. ⁸ [⁹] And when the fourteen days of the wedding feast <note>referring to the extended celebration mentioned on the previous page.</note> which Raguel had sworn to keep were completed, Tobias went to him and said: "Let me go; for I know that my father and my mother believe they will never see me again. Now therefore I beg you, father, to let me go, that I may go to my father; I have already explained to you the state in which I left him." ⁹ [⁸] And Raguel said to Tobias: "Remain here with me, and I will send messengers to your father, and they will tell him about you." And he said: "Not at all, father, I beg that you let me go from here to my father." ¹⁰ And immediately rising, Raguel delivered to Tobias Sarah his daughter, and half of all his substance <term>substance: wealth including property and livestock</term>, male and female servants, sheep and oxen, donkeys and camels, clothing, vessels, and money: and he sent him away safe and sound, and bid him farewell, ¹¹ and said: "Go, my son, safe and sound; may the Lord of heaven dispose your journey well, and that of Sarah your wife, and may I see children from you before I die." ¹² And he took hold of her, and greeting her, he kissed Sarah his daughter, ¹³ and said to her: "Daughter, have honor for your father-in-law and your mother-in-law; they from now on are your parents, just as your own father and mother, who [bore] you... <note>The text breaks off here mid-sentence.</note>

->## CHAPTER XI<-

¹ And they set out, and they traveled until they came to Charam <note>likely Haran, or a location near the approach to Nineveh</note>, which is a city opposite Nineveh. ² Then Raphael said: "Tobias, brother, do you not know in what state you left your father? ³ Let us go ahead of your wife, and let us go and prepare the house, while the girl follows us." ⁴ And they both went ahead together, and the angel said to him: "Take some of that gall <note>referring to the fish gall mentioned in earlier chapters</note> with you, and keep it." And the dog also went with them. ⁵ And Anna <note>Tobit's wife/Tobias's mother</note> sat by the road, watching for the arrival of her son. ⁶ And she recognized him coming, and said to his father: "Behold, your son is coming, and the man who was with him." ⁷ And Raphael said to Tobias: "Before we reach your father, I know indeed that his eyes shall be opened; ⁸ therefore, sprinkle the fish gall <term>fel piscis: the bile of a fish, used here as a divinely-directed medicinal agent</term> upon his eyes, and the medicine will act; and you will peel away the white scales <term>albugines: literally "whiteness," referring to the cataracts or film covering Tobit's eyes</term> from his eyes, and your father will look up and see the light." ⁹ And his mother ran to him, and fell upon her son's neck, and said to him: "Son, I see you; from now on I can die content"; and she wept. Tobias also wept. ¹⁰ And Tobit rose up, stumbling with his feet; and he went out of the courtyard, and Tobias met him, carrying the fish gall in his hands. ¹¹ And he blew into the eyes of Tobit his father, and took hold of him, and said to him: "Be of good courage, father!"

...bless the Lord of ages, so that his tabernacle <note>A "tabernacle" refers to a dwelling place or sanctuary, specifically the place where God’s presence resides.</note> may be built again in you with joy, and that he may make all the captives joyful within you, and love all the wretched forever and ever. <sup>13</sup> They shall shine with a bright light in all the ends of the earth. <sup>14</sup> Many nations shall come to you from afar, to dwell from the farthest parts of the earth because of the name of the Lord my God; and having their gifts in their hands, offering them in gladness to the King of heaven and earth. <sup>15</sup> And your name shall be great forever and ever. <sup>16</sup> Cursed are all who despise you, and all who blaspheme you: cursed shall be all who hate you, and all who speak a harsh word, and all who cast you down and destroy your walls, and all who overthrow your towers, and all who burn your dwellings: and blessed shall be all who build you up, for all time. <sup>17</sup> Then rejoice and be glad in the children of the righteous, for they shall all be gathered together and shall bless the eternal Lord. <sup>18</sup> Happy are those who love you, and who rejoice in your peace. Blessed are all the people who shall be sorrowful over all your scourges <note>original: "flagellis" — refers to divine punishments or trials meant to correct and purify.</note>, for they shall rejoice in you and see all your joy forever. <sup>19</sup> O my soul, bless the Lord, the great King, for he has delivered Jerusalem, and will build his house in her again forever and ever. <sup>20</sup> I shall be happy if any of my descendants <note>original: "reliquiae de semine meo" — literally, the "remnant of my seed."</note> remain to see your brightness and to give thanks to the name of the King of heaven. <sup>21</sup> The gates of Jerusalem shall be built of sapphire and emerald: and all your walls of precious stone, and the towers of Jerusalem shall be built with gold, and her battlements <note>original: "propugnacula" — defensive fortifications.</note> with pure gold. <sup>22</sup> And the streets of Jerusalem shall be paved with carbuncle <note>A "carbuncle" is an archaic term for a glowing red gemstone, usually a garnet.</note> stones: and her gates shall sing a song of joy, and all her lanes shall speak. <sup>23</sup> Blessed be the Lord, who exalts you, and blessed forever and ever: because in you he shall bless his holy name for all time.

In that day, there shall be joy, and all righteousness shall be among them, ⁹ and those who love the true God shall rejoice; but those who commit iniquity and sin shall vanish from all the lands. ¹⁰ And now, my children, I command you: Serve the Lord in truth, and do before Him what is pleasing to Him. ¹¹ And command your children that they perform acts of righteousness and charity, and that they be mindful of Him, and bless His name at all times, in truth, with all their heart, and with all their strength. ¹² Now indeed, my son, depart from Nineveh..."

# APPENDIX.

## A. BEL AND THE DRAGON. <note>This title refers to a famous narrative usually included as an addition to the Book of Daniel. It describes how Daniel exposes the fraud of the priests of the idol Bel and kills a sacred dragon, proving the power of the living God.</note>

The <term>Midrash Rabbah de Rabbah</term> <note>A "Midrash" is an ancient Jewish method of interpreting and expanding upon biblical verses through stories, parables, and legal analysis. "Rabbah de Rabbah" implies a particularly extensive or prestigious collection of these teachings.</note> on the verse "And they cast him into the pit" (Genesis 37:24), in the section beginning "And Jacob dwelt" (Genesis 37:1), says: "I called upon your name, O Lord, out of the low dungeon" (Lamentations 3:55). This refers to Daniel, who prayed before the Holy One (blessed is he) <note>original: "baruch hu" — a standard Jewish honorific used whenever mentioning God.</note> out of the pit, and the Holy One (blessed is he) heard the voice of his prayer, and delivered him from the lions. This is what is written, "You have heard my voice," and "You drew near in the day that I called upon you," and so on (Lamentations 3:56, 57). And so he says, "My God has sent his angel, and has shut the lions' mouths, so that they have not hurt me, forasmuch as before him innocence was found in me" (Daniel 6:23).

And it was not sufficient that he delivered him, but he also avenged him on his enemies. This is what is written, "O Lord, you have pleaded the causes of my soul, you have redeemed my life" (Lamentations 3:58). And so he says, "And the king commanded, and they brought those men who had accused Daniel, and they cast them into the den of lions—them, their children, and their wives; and the lions had the mastery of them, and broke all their bones in pieces before they even reached the bottom of the den" (Daniel 6:25). Behold, we find from Scripture that Daniel was delivered from the pit, and our <term>Rabbis</term> <note>The ancient Jewish scholars and teachers who preserved and interpreted the religious laws and traditions.</note> say that we have a tradition that Daniel was delivered a second time from the lions’ pit in the days of Cyrus the Persian, because he would not believe in the worship of idols. They say <note>For the translation of the Syriac text, see the Preface, page xiv, note 1.</note>. Therefore <note>This text corresponds to page 43 of the original manuscript.</note> it is said, "They have cut off my life in the pit, and..."

<summary>This page introduces an appendix concerning the story of Bel and the Dragon, using a Jewish Midrash to link Daniel's deliverance from the lions to broader themes of divine justice and the rejection of idolatry.</summary>
<keywords>Bel and the Dragon, Daniel, Midrash Rabbah, lions' den, Cyrus the Persian, idolatry, Lamentations, Genesis</keywords>

<page-num>xcii</page-num>

->EXTRACT FROM THE <term>B'RESHITH RABBAH</term><note>Also known as "Genesis Rabbah," this is a collection of ancient Jewish interpretations and stories focused on the Book of Genesis.</note>.<-

cast a stone upon me. Waters flowed over mine head; then I said, I am cut off. I called upon thy name, O Lord, out of the low pit' (Lamentations 3:53–55). We find that Daniel was twice cast into the pit, once in the days of Darius the Mede, and another time in the days of Cyrus the Persian, his father-in-law. Now on the first occasion he remained only one night in the pit, and was delivered; but on the second he remained in it seven days, and was delivered. This second history is not written in the sacred books <note>The "sacred books" here refers to the Hebrew Bible (Tanakh) as recognized in the Jewish tradition.</note>. It would seem that, inasmuch as the righteous man is used to this miracle, it is sufficient to mention it once.

These two histories <note>An addition by the copyist of the manuscript.</note>, that is, the history of Bel, the image of the Chaldeans, and the story of the Dragon, are included by the Christians in the twenty-four books of the Prophets <note>This refers to the traditional count of books in the Old Testament; while Jews and Protestants consider these stories "Apocrypha," they were historically included in the expanded Christian canon.</note>, together with three other books. The translator says that he has not found them in the sacred tongue <note>original: "leshona qadisha" — referring to Hebrew, the original language of the Jewish scriptures.</note>.

### B. EXTRACT FROM THE GENESIS RABBAH, § 68.

ANOTHER explanation. 'And behold the angels of God.' This is Daniel. 'Ascending and descending on it.' For he went up and brought forth from inside its mouth what it had swallowed. This is what the Scripture says: 'And I will punish Bel in Babylon, and I will bring forth out of his mouth that which he hath swallowed up' (Jeremiah 51:44). For Nebuchadnezzar <note>The powerful king of the Neo-Babylonian Empire who famously destroyed the First Temple in Jerusalem.</note> had a dragon, which used to swallow up everything which they cast before it. Nebuchadnezzar said to Daniel, How great is its power, for it swallows up everything which they cast before it. Daniel said to him, Give me permission and I will weaken it. So he gave him permission. What did he do? He took straw, and hid nails in the midst thereof; then he cast it before it, and the nails pierced its intestines. This is what the Scripture says: 'And I will bring forth out of his mouth that which he hath swallowed up.'

->EXTRACT FROM THE <term>B'RESHITH RABBAH</term><note>Also known as "Genesis Rabbah," this is a foundational collection of ancient Jewish parables and legal interpretations (Midrash) based on the Book of Genesis, compiled around the 5th century CE.</note>.<-

...cast a stone upon me. Waters flowed over mine head; then I said, I am cut off. I called upon thy name, O Lord, out of the low pit" (Lamentations 3:53–55). We find that Daniel was twice cast into the pit: once in the days of Darius the Mede, and another time in the days of Cyrus the Persian, his son-in-law <note>Ancient traditions often harmonized the genealogies of the Persian and Median kings to link these biblical figures.</note>. On the first occasion, he remained there only one night and was delivered; <margin>5</margin> but on the second, he remained in it for seven days and was delivered. This second account is not written in the Sacred Books <note>Referring to the Hebrew Bible (Tanakh). This story appears in the "Apocrypha," books excluded from the Jewish canon but kept in many Christian Bibles.</note>. It appears that, because the righteous man was already experienced in this miracle, it was sufficient for the scriptures to mention it once.

These two accounts—namely, the story of Bel, the idol of the Chaldeans, and the story of the Dragon—are included by the Christians among the twenty-four books of the Prophets, <margin>10</margin> along with three other books. The translator of those works stated that he did not find them written in the Holy Tongue <note>original: "Leshon Ha-Kodesh" — referring to Hebrew, the original language of the Bible.</note>.

## B. EXTRACT FROM THE GENESIS RABBAH,
## § 68.

Another explanation: "And behold, the Lord stood above him" (Genesis 28:13). "And behold, the angels of God"—this refers to Daniel, who "ascends and descends" in this context. For he went up and removed that which was swallowed from the mouth of the idol, as it is written: "And I will punish Bel in Babylon, and I will bring forth out of his mouth that which he hath swallowed" (Jeremiah 51:44). King Nebuchadnezzar had a certain dragon <note>Often depicted as a giant, sacred serpent worshipped as a living god in Babylon.</note> that would <margin>15</margin> swallow everything they threw before it. Nebuchadnezzar said to Daniel: "See how great its power is, for it swallows all that is cast before it!" Daniel replied: "Give me permission, and I will weaken it." The King gave him permission. What did Daniel do? He took straw, hid nails inside it, and threw it before the dragon. The nails pierced its entrails <note>original: "benei me'av" — literally "the sons of his intestines."</note>. This is the meaning of the verse: "And I will bring forth out of his mouth that which he hath swallowed."

### BEL AND THE DRAGON.

And they gathered against the king and turned against him, saying to one another, "The king has become a Jew! He has smashed Bel, killed the dragon, and slain the priests." They came to the king and said to him, "Either deliver Daniel to us, or we will kill you and your household." The king saw that they all rose up against him at once; he was pressured and, by compulsion, he delivered Daniel to them.

<margin>5</margin> They took Daniel <note>One ancient source, the *Dagger of Faith* (Pugio Fidei), suggests a reading of "and they went and cast him in."</note> and cast him into the pit of lions, and he was there for six days. They covered the opening with a stone and sealed it with the king’s signet ring and with their own signet rings. He remained there for six days so that the lions might be famished and devour him. Now, there were seven lions in the pit, and they were usually given two carcasses and two rams <note>Aramaic: *dukhrin*</note> every day. But during those days, they were given nothing to eat, <margin>10</margin> so that they would be famished and devour Daniel. 

At that time, the prophet Habakkuk was in Judea; he had cooked a stew and crumbled bread into a bowl, and was heading to the field to bring it to his reapers. But the angel of the Lord said to him, "Go, carry this meal you have to Daniel, who is cast into the lions’ pit in Babylon." Habakkuk replied, "Lord, I have never seen Babylon, and I do not know where the pit is." Then the angel placed his hand upon the head of Habakkuk and took him up by the hair of his head, <margin>15</margin> setting him down in Babylon above the pit by the power of the Holy Spirit. 

Habakkuk called out and said, "Daniel! Daniel! Arise and take this meal which God has sent to you." Daniel said, "O God, You have remembered me and have not withheld Your mercy from me; I know that You do not forsake those who love You." Then Daniel arose and ate. In that same hour, the angel took Habakkuk and returned him to the place where he had taken him, just as he was before. 

<margin>20</margin> On the seventh day, the king came to weep for Daniel, for he was deeply concerned for him. He approached the pit and looked in, and he saw Daniel sitting there. The king cried out with a loud voice, saying, "Great is the God of Daniel!" 

He brought Daniel up out of the pit, but as for Daniel's enemies <note>The *Dagger of Faith* also identifies these as his legal "adversaries" or "accusers."</note>—those who had slandered him <note>literally "ate his pieces," a Semitic idiom for malicious accusation</note> and sought to kill him—the king cast them into the pit. In that very hour, the lions devoured them in the presence of the king and in the presence of Daniel.

### ⸪ Concerning the matter of the Dragon, which is called the Burden of the Dragon ⸪

<margin>2</margin> There was also a great dragon there which the Babylonians worshipped. The king said to Daniel, "Behold, <note>Aramaic: *Hanuna* (Behold/This one). Some versions read *Hasha* (Now).</note> you cannot say of this one that it is not a living god, so worship 

<margin>15</margin> it, for it is a living god." Daniel said to the king, "I worship the Lord my God, for He is the living God. But you, O king, give me authority over it, and I will kill this dragon without sword or staff." The king said, "It is granted to you."

Then Daniel took pitch, hair, and fat, and boiled them together. He made cakes <term>asphiri<gloss>small round balls or spheres, from the Greek 'sphaia'</gloss></term> from them and threw them into the mouth of the dragon; it burst open and died. And it happened that when the Babylonians heard this, they were very distressed.

# BEL AND THE DRAGON.

Every day [the king] would worship it, but Daniel worshipped God. And the king said to Daniel, "Why do you not worship Bel, my god?" Daniel said to him, "I do not worship an idol, nor pottery, nor carvings, because they are the work of human hands; rather, I fear the living God, He who made heaven and earth and is the <term>Lord over all flesh</term> <note>Syriac: *mare 'al kol bsar*. This title emphasizes God's absolute sovereignty over every living being, contrasting with the inanimate idol.</note>; Him I worship."

<margin>5</margin> And the king said to Daniel, "Does it not seem to you that Bel is alive? Or do you not see that he eats and drinks every day?" Then Daniel laughed and said to the king, "Do not be deceived, O king! <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original Aramaic: *hna gar men lgo d-tina hu* (For this one is clay from within). The gloss notes that the Polyglot version explicitly mentions the internal clay.</note> For inside it is clay, and outside it is bronze, and never in its days has it eaten or drunk."

Then the king was enraged and called the <term>priests</term> <note>Aramaic: *kumre*. In this context, it refers specifically to the sacrificial priests of the Babylonian temple.</note> of Bel and said to them, "If you do not tell me who consumes this entire <margin>10</margin> expenditure, you shall die; but if you show me that Bel eats it, Daniel shall die because he has blasphemed against Bel." Daniel said, "Let it be according to your word, O king."

Now there were seventy priests of Bel, apart from their wives and children. And the king came, he and Daniel, to the house of Bel. And the priests said to the king, "Behold, we are going outside; and you <gloss>2</gloss> <note>original: *So. Plg. it* (There is/You are). The scribe notes a variation in the verb form in the Polyglot version.</note>, O king, set out the food for Bel and mix the wine and set it; then shut the door and seal it with your signet ring. And come early in the morning; if <margin>15</margin> you do not find that Bel has eaten all of the food, we shall die; but if it is Bel who has eaten it, Daniel shall die, <gloss>3</gloss> <note>original: *Plg. hu* (he). A minor grammatical variant referring to Daniel.</note> who has blasphemed against us."

And they [the priests] were confident because they had made a hidden entrance beneath the table, and through it they would enter and frequent the place and eat everything that was placed for Bel and take what was left over.

When the priests of Bel had gone out, the king brought the food and filled the table <margin>20</margin> and placed it before Bel, and filled the wine vessels according to their custom. And when he had gone out, Daniel said to the <term>servants</term> <note>Aramaic: *talye*. These are Daniel's young attendants or pages.</note>, "Bring me ashes," and he scattered them with a sieve before the king throughout the entire house of Bel. And the king saw it and went out; and he shut the door and sealed it with his signet ring and with Daniel’s signet ring.

And the priests went in the night according to their custom, they and their wives and their children, and they ate everything that was set for Bel and drank the wine. And the king rose early...

<meta>This page begins an appendix containing an Aramaic version of the story of "Bel and the Dragon." Although the previous page (41) described the climax of Daniel’s trial with the idol, this text serves as a supplementary source or a textual variant, providing the traditional Midrashic introduction and the opening setup of the narrative. It seeks to harmonize the biblical account of Daniel in the lions' den with this additional story of the idol's destruction.</meta>

# APPENDIX.

## A. BEL AND THE DRAGON.

Midrash Rabbah <note>A collection of ancient Jewish homiletic interpretations of the Torah. "Rabbah" means "Great."</note> on the portion *Vayishev Yaakov* <note>The section of Genesis beginning at chapter 37, verse 1.</note> regarding the verse: **"And they cast him into the pit"**: "I called upon Your name, O Lord, from the lowest pit" [Lamentations 3:55]. This refers to Daniel, who prayed before <margin>5</margin> the Holy One, Blessed be He, from the pit; and the Holy One, Blessed be He, heard the voice of his prayer and saved him from the lions. This is as it is written: "You heard my voice..." [Lamentations 3:56] and "You drew near on the day I called to you..." [Lamentations 3:57]. And likewise it says: "My God sent His angel and shut the lions' mouths, and they have not harmed me, because I was found innocent before Him" [Daniel 6:23]. And not only <margin>10</margin> did He save him, but He took vengeance on his enemies, as it says: "O Lord, You have pleaded the causes of my soul; You have redeemed my life" [Lamentations 3:58]. And likewise it says: "And the king commanded, and they brought those men who had accused Daniel, and they cast them into the den of lions—them, their children, and their wives; and the lions overpowered them and broke all their bones in pieces before they reached the bottom of the den" [Daniel 6:25]. <margin>15</margin> Behold, we find that Daniel was saved from the pit according to the Scripture. But our Sages said: It is a tradition in our hands that at another time Daniel was saved from the lions' pit in the days of Cyrus the Persian, because he denied the idol and destroyed it.

->They said:<-

King Astyages¹ <margin>¹ הוא דריוש המדי <note>original Hebrew margin: "He is Darius the Mede." The scribe is identifying the Median king mentioned here with the Darius found in the biblical Book of Daniel.</note></margin> was gathered to his ancestors, and Cyrus the Persian received his kingdom. And Daniel was honored in the presence of the king and was with the king, and he was praised more than all of them. And the Babylonians had a certain idol whose name was Bel, and every day the expenditure for it was twelve <term>ardabim</term> <note>original Aramaic: *ardabin*. A large unit of measure for dry goods like grain, roughly equivalent to 50 liters or 1.5 bushels.</note> of fine flour, forty rams, and six <term>measures</term> <note>original Aramaic: *metrin*. Derived from the Greek *metretes*, a liquid measure of about 9 gallons.</note> of wine. And the king worshipped it and went to it daily.

<summary>This page introduces an Aramaic version of the Bel and the Dragon story, prefacing it with a Midrashic commentary that links Daniel's biblical ordeal in the lions' den to his destruction of the Babylonian idol. It establishes the setting under King Cyrus and describes the massive daily offerings used to maintain the illusion that the idol Bel was alive.</summary>

# ADDITION TO THE MIDRASH THANḤUMÂ.
<note>Midrash Thanḥumâ is a collection of ancient Jewish homilies (sermons) organized according to the weekly Torah portions. It is named after Rabbi Thanḥumâ bar Abba, a renowned 4th-century scholar.</note>

"And no business shall pass upon him; he shall be free for his house for one year, and shall cheer his wife whom he has taken," <note>original: "ולא יעבור עליו לכל דבר נקי יהיה לביתו שנה אחת ושמח את אשתו אשר לקח" — This is a direct quote from the Book of Deuteronomy 24:5.</note> and the <term>Holy One, Blessed be He<gloss>Hebrew: HaKadosh Baruch Hu; the standard way of referring to God in Jewish tradition</gloss></term> is Truth and His <term>Torah<gloss>The foundational law and divine instruction</gloss></term> is Truth. When they heard the groom and the bride laughing and rejoicing together, they entered the room to see the matter; they saw and rejoiced. They made the matter known to the community and gave thanks to the Name <note>A common Jewish practice of using "The Name" (HaShem) to avoid pronouncing the sacred name of God.</note>. And this is how the Holy One, Blessed be He, protects those who trust in Him.

"...reject her, and I will give you much silver and gold, for you are a handsome and wise young man, and by my advice, do not endanger yourself with her." He prepared a <term>bridal chamber<gloss>Hebrew: "כילה" (Kilah) — a canopy or curtained area for the wedding night</gloss></term>, and the groom sat within it. When you sit down to dine, a poor man will come to you dressed in black and torn garments, barefoot and weary, 
<margin>10</margin> with hair as stiff as nails—a poor man like whom there is none else in all the world. When you see him, rise from your seat, seat him beside you, and feed him and give him drink. Serve him with all your might and honor him. Do not let a single word fall of all that I have spoken to you, and you shall remain in peace. Now I will go my way." 
The old man departed, and the groom returned to his place. They sat at the feast, and when they began to eat, that 
poor man arrived. When the groom saw him, he rose from his place and did for him everything that the old man had commanded.

your time has already arrived." He said to him, "I beg of you, wait for me until I go and take leave 
from my wife." He replied, "Regarding this matter, I will grant your request; go and come back quickly." He went to the room where she 
<margin>20</margin> was sitting alone, weeping and praying to her Creator. The young man opened the room and called to her. 
She came to open it for him, brought him into her room, took hold of him, and kissed him. She said 
to him, "My brother, why have you come?" He said to her, "To take leave of you, for my time has come to go the way 
of all the earth <note>A biblical idiom for dying.</note>, for the angel has come and told me that he has come to seek my soul." She said to him, "You shall 
not go! Instead, sit here, and I will go to him and speak with him." She went and found him.
