## ->And thus it is found in the book of the Rabbi, R. Moshe the Preacher<-

"He protected him as the apple of His eye"—the Holy One, blessed be He, protects those He has tested just as a person protects the apple of his eye. 
There is a story of a man, very wealthy and well-regarded, who had an only daughter of very beautiful form and great piety. She was married three times to three different men, and on the first night of each marriage, the next morning the husband would be found dead. She said, "No more men shall die 
<margin>5</margin> because of me; I will remain a widow and <term>bound<gloss>Hebrew: "עגונה" (Agunah) — typically a woman "chained" to a marriage she cannot exit; here used to mean she will remain secluded and unmarried</gloss></term> until <term>the Place<gloss>Hebrew: "המקום" (Ha-Makom) — A traditional Rabbinic title for God, implying His presence is everywhere</gloss></term> sees and has mercy." She remained so for many days. Now, that wealthy man had a brother who was very poor living in another land, and he had ten sons. Every single day, he and his eldest son would bring bundles of wood from the forest and sell them, and from this, he, his wife, and his sons were sustained. Once, they did not sell any and they had no money to buy bread, and they did not eat that day. The next 
<margin>10</margin> day, the same happened to them; they went into the forest and the father’s spirit grew faint. The son’s eyes overflowed with tears for the poor <note>meaning his own family's poverty</note> and he lifted his eyes to the heavens. The son reflected in his heart, took leave of his father and mother, and went to the land where his uncle lived. When he arrived at the house, his uncle rejoiced over him with great joy, as did his wife and daughter. They asked him about his father, his mother, and the children. He stayed with him for seven days. At the end of 
<margin>15</margin> seven days, the young man came to his uncle and said, "I have one request to ask of you; do not turn me away." His uncle said to him, "Speak, my son, whatever you wish." He said, "Swear to me [to grant it]," and he did so. He said, "This is the request I ask of you: that you give me your daughter as a wife." When the man heard this, he wept and said, "No, my son, do not! For because of my sins, such is her fate <note>referring to the supernatural death of her previous husbands</note>." He said to him, "I ask even on those terms." He said to him, "If it is for the sake of my money that you are jumping at her, do not..."

### HEBREW TRANSLATION. CH. X.

"I cannot go there because of the oath that Raguel swore to me, that I should not leave his house until fourteen days have passed. Meanwhile, my father and my mother are counting the days, and if the time is exceeded by even one day, I will cause my parents' souls to grieve." <note>Tobias is caught between his duty to his new father-in-law, who insisted on a two-week wedding feast, and his elderly parents back in Nineveh.</note> And **Raphael** <note>The angel, still disguised as a traveling companion.</note> arose and took two camels and four servants and went to Rages <note>original: "Ranaush," a likely corruption of Rages (Rhages) in Media</note> to the house of **Gabael**. He gave him his bond <note>A receipt or token for the silver deposited years earlier.</note> <margin>5</margin> and told him that Tobias son of Tobit had taken **Sarah** daughter of Raguel as a wife, and he [Raphael] invited him to come to the wedding feast of Tobias. Then Gabael loaded the silver onto the camels and came to the wedding feast. He found Tobias sitting at the table, and he embraced him and kissed him and wept with him out of great joy. He blessed him and said, "Blessed is the **LORD** God of Israel who has joined you in joy with this woman. But she refused to be comforted and went out to the roads every day to see if her son was coming. She tasted nothing but tears day and night. When the fourteen days of the wedding feast were completed, Tobias said to **Raguel**, <margin>15</margin> "Send me away, for my father and my mother are counting the days and do not expect [to see me again]." But Raguel said, "Stay a while longer with me, and I will send word to tell your father all that you have done." Tobias replied, "Do not delay me; send me away that I may go to my father." Then Raguel gave his daughter Sarah to Tobias, along with half of his wealth: servants and maidservants, sheep and cattle, donkeys and camels, garments of fine linen and purple, and vessels of silver and gold. # CH. V. BOOK OF TOBIT.

the heavens, <note>This sentence begins mid-thought from the previous page, likely referring to the God of the Heavens.</note> and you, my son, keep yourself from all impurity and from all fornication. Take for yourself a wife from your own family, and not from any foreign people who are not of the lineage of your fathers; for we are the children of the prophets. Remember, my son, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, who took wives from their own families and were not willing to intermarry with foreigners; and so they were blessed with sons
<margin>5</margin> and daughters. And you, my son, set your heart to all your deeds; and what you hate for yourself, do not do to others. <note>This is a famous negative formulation of the "Golden Rule," appearing here in the Book of Tobit long before similar famous attributions in other Jewish traditions.</note> Do not let the wages of a hired worker stay with you overnight *from the day you agree <note>original: tistakem</note> with him;
the Lord will pay you your own reward. Keep yourself from drunkenness, and let no iniquity befall you. Give your bread to the hungry, and with your garments cover the naked. From all your abundance, give
charity, and do not let it be difficult in your eyes. *Pour out your bread and wine on the graves of the righteous,* <note>This refers to a practice of giving food to the poor at the burial sites of the righteous, or perhaps an ancient funerary custom that the text encourages as an act of piety.</note>
<margin>10</margin> and hear and receive good counsel from anyone who gives it to you. At all times, ask of the Lord, and He will make your paths straight and your counsel successful; for no man possesses counsel of his own, rather it is in the hands of the Holy One, Blessed be He <note>A common Jewish honorific for God, often abbreviated as "H-B-H".</note>
(alone), for He does whatever He desires; He brings this one low and He raises that one up. Keep my words and all that I have commanded you, and let them not depart from your eyes. ## II. HEBREW TRANSLATION.

### [ ∴ Book of Tobit ∴ ]

->1<-

This is the book of Tobit, son of Tobiel, son of Hananel (son of Ariel), son of *Gabael, son of* <note>The Paris manuscript (P) reads "Gabriel, son of"; however, all other manuscripts use the name "Nebiel" in almost every instance.</note> Asael, son of Nathaniel <note>Spelled "Gnatniel" in the Paris manuscript.</note> from the tribe of Naphtali (who was exiled from Samaria with the exile that was carried away in the days of Hoshea, son of Elah), who was exiled in the days of Shalmaneser <note>Spelled "Shalmalaser" throughout the Paris manuscript.</note> king of Assyria. He was among the inhabitants <note>All sources agree on this reading, though the printed edition suggests "or he stayed."</note> of the city of Naphtali, which is in Galilee on the border of the sea. And Tobit said:
<margin>5</margin> "Remember me, O my God, for good <note>original: "letovah," a traditional Hebrew plea for divine favor</note> because I walked before You all the days of my life in the way of integrity, performing many righteous acts and good deeds of kindness for my brothers and for my people in the exile at Nineveh in the land of Assyria. And it happened that when I was a youth in the land of Israel, the entire tribe of Naphtali rebelled against the House of David <note>The printed edition adds the phrase "king of Israel" here.</note> and they stopped going up to Jerusalem—*the city which the Lord chose from all the tribes of Israel, where the altar of the Lord was sanctified for all the tribes of Israel, and where the Temple of the Lord was built to offer* <note>The Paris manuscript reads "and to offer."</note> <margin>10</margin> burnt offerings and peace offerings to the Lord three times a year. Yet all my brothers <note>One manuscript (M) reads "my men."</note> of the tribe of Naphtali were sacrificing sacrifices and burnt offerings to the golden calves which Jeroboam, son of Nebat, the king of Israel, had made in Bethel and in Dan. But I would go to Jerusalem during the festivals, as it is written in the Torah of the Lord concerning Israel, bringing firstfruits, tithes, and the firstborn [animals] for the priests, the sons of Aaron, along with grain, new wine, <margin>15</margin> oil, figs, pomegranates, and all the fruit of the ground for the sons of Levi who minister before the Lord in Jerusalem. I also brought the second tithe and the third tithe for the stranger, the orphan, and the widow (and I went...</note>

<summary>This page introduces the genealogy of Tobit and establishes his character as a devout man who remained loyal to the Jerusalem Temple and the laws of the Torah despite his tribe's rebellion and their subsequent exile to Assyria.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the narrative of Tobit and Tobiah, following the healing of Tobit’s blindness and the return of Tobiah with his new wife, Sarah. # CH. XII. HISTORY OF TOBIT.

And he <note>Tobiah</note> did not find Raphael, though he asked every person in the city about him; yet he did not find a single man who had seen him. He returned to his father and said to him, “I did not find him.” Then his father knew that it was the angel Raphael whom God had sent to heal Sarah from the hand of the demon <note>referring to Asmodeus, the demon who killed Sarah's previous husbands</note> and to heal his own eyes. And he blessed God and said: “Blessed be God who sent His good angel with my son and made his way successful and healed two <term>afflicted ones</term> <note>original: mar'in</note> <gloss>1</gloss> <note>The manuscript reads "mardin" (rebellious ones) here, though "mar'in" (sick/afflicted ones) is the likely intended meaning.</note> at that time.” And from 그날 forward,
<margin>5</margin> God prospered Tobit and Tobiah his son; He gave him children from Sarah his wife. Then Raguel and Edna his wife died, and Tobiah inherited all their possessions.

After some days, Tobit became ill, and he called Tobiah his son and commanded him regarding the commandments of God. He said to him: “My son, be one who performs <term>acts of kindness</term> <note>original: gamil hasda</note> all your days with the poor and the rich,
<margin>10</margin> and perform <term>charity</term> <note>original: tzedakah, which implies both social justice and financial almsgiving</note> all your days. For because of this, God will bless all the work of your hands. Abraham our father, because of the charity and the <term>tithe</term> <note>original: ma'asra, the practice of giving a tenth of one's income to the poor or the temple</note> that he gave, the Lord blessed him. And likewise Isaac, because he gave tithes and performed charity. And likewise Jacob, when he went to the house of Laban and prayed his prayer, he did not
vow anything except to give the tithe and charity to the poor; and because of this, God made him successful and gave
him all that he asked and protected him from Laban and from Esau his brother. And you, if you do
<margin>15</margin> according to their works, He will bless you just as He blessed them.” And he commanded them regarding the rest of the commandments, and finished giving his charge, and was <term>gathered to his people</term> <note>A biblical idiom meaning he died and joined his ancestors in the afterlife.</note>. Tobiah his son buried him with great honor. After his death, God blessed Tobiah because he fulfilled the commandments of his father, and He prospered him greatly and placed a blessing in all the work of his hands.

Behold, we have learned how great is the power of charities and tithes. Because Tobit performed
<margin>20</margin> charities and set aside his tithes as is proper, see how the <term>Holy One, Blessed be He</term> <note>original: Ha-Kadosh Barukh Hu, a common Jewish title for God</note> rewarded him. And because the <term>Patriarchs of the world</term> <note>original: Avot Ha-Olam, referring to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob</note> knew the power of charity and tithes, they were careful with them. Regarding Abraham, it is written: “And he gave him a tithe of everything.” Regarding Isaac, it is written: “And Isaac sowed in that land.” And "sowing" is nothing other than charity, as it was said: “Sow for yourselves for charity.” Regarding Jacob, it is written: “And of all that You give me, I will surely give a tenth to You.”

->❖ Thus ends the story of Tobiah; praise be to God. ❖<-

# CHALDEE TEXT. CHAPTER VII.

Raphael said to him, "Do you not remember your father’s command, who commanded you that you should take a wife from your father’s lineage? And now, listen to me and do not fear the <term>demon: Ashmedai or Asmodeus</term>. I know that you shall take her this night as a wife. And when you enter the <term>chamber: the bridal suite or inner room</term> with her, take the heart of the fish and cause smoke from it under her garment; the demon will smell it and he will flee and never return. ### CH. VI. HISTORY OF TOBIT.

In peace: Tobit called Tobiah his son and said to him, "My son, prepare yourself and go on the way with your brother. May the God of heaven guide you both there in peace, and may He send His angel with you, and may He make your way prosper and return you in peace." Tobiah kissed his father and his mother, and they said to him, "Go in peace." And they went out to walk. But his mother wept and said to Tobit, "Why <margin>5</margin> did you not fear to send the youth away? For he is our only son, and he should go out and come in before us. Without silver, God has provided for us." <note>original: כספא — literally "money" or "silver"; she is arguing that their son is more precious than the money they are going to retrieve.</note> Tobit said to her, "Do not fear; in peace he shall go and in peace he shall return, and a good angel shall go with him and prosper his way, and your eyes shall see him as he returns in peace; so refrain from weeping."

-> ו <- <note>This letter 'Waw' serves as a section marker or catchword.</note>

<margin>10</margin> The youth went, and Raphael with him, and they came to the Tigris river <note>original: נחל תיגרין — the River Tigris</note> in the evening and stayed there. Tobiah ran to the river to wash his feet, and a certain fish came out of the river and ate the youth’s bread. <note>original: ואכל לחמא — In many versions of Tobit, the fish attacks his foot; here the Aramaic specifies it "ate the bread."</note> The youth cried out. Raphael said to him, "Seize the fish and do not let it go!" And he took hold of the fish and brought it up to the dry land. Raphael said to him, "Split the fish in the middle and take its heart; it is good to make smoke from it before a man who has a demonic spirit or an <margin>15</margin> evil spirit in him, and they will flee from him. And take the gall <note>original: ומררתא — the bile or gall bladder</note> to smear upon eyes that have white spots <note>original: חיווא — likely referring to leukoma or cataracts</note> and they shall be healed." Tobiah did so; he took the heart and the gall, and he roasted the fish and ate it, and the remainder he left on the way. And they went until Media and arrived in Ecbatana. <note>original: אגבתנים — Ecbatana, the capital of Media.</note> Raphael said to Tobiah, "My brother, you shall come to Raguel, for he is an old man, and he has one daughter who is very beautiful, and her name is Sarah. I will tell him to give her to you as a wife, for she is the only child of her father, and he loves her <margin>20</margin> greatly. She is a good woman and fears Heaven. And when we return from Rages <note>original: ראגיש — the city where the silver was deposited</note> we will perform the marriage canopy <note>original: חופתא — the huppah or wedding rite</note>, and I know that Raguel will not refuse your request, but will give her to you, and we shall bring her with us to your father." Tobiah said to Raphael, "I have heard that she was given to seven men and they died before they came in to her. I have heard that Ashmedai, the king of the demons, kills them. And now I am afraid of the demon, lest he kill me and I bring the gray hair of my father [and mother] <margin>25</margin> in sorrow to the grave <note>original: לשאול — to Sheol, the place of the dead</note>, for they have no other free son nor a daughter to bury them at their death."

### CHALDEE TEXT. CH. V.
### THE HISTORY OF TOBIT

The wise men realized that he was an angel of the Lord. He asked him: "From where have you come?" He replied: "From the children of Israel, one of your brothers." Tobiah said to him: "Do you know the way to go to Media?" The angel said to him: "I know the way, and I have stayed in Media; I was a guest with <note>original: אושפיזא — a guest or host; from the same root as 'hospice'.</note> <sup>1</sup> Gabael <note>The manuscript uses the word 'also' here, likely a scribe's error for 'Gabael'.</note> our brother who lives in Ecbatana <note>original: באגבתנים — Ecbatana, the ancient capital of Media.</note> <sup>2</sup> near <note>There appears to be an omission in the source text between Ecbatana and Rages.</note> Rages; it is built on a mountain, while Ecbatana is in the plain." Tobiah said to him: "Wait 
<margin>5</margin> for me a little, and I will tell my father, for I desire that you go with me, and I will give you the wages for the journey." He said to him: "I will wait until you come." Tobiah went and told his father: "I have found a man, one of our brothers, who will go with me." Tobit said to him: "Go, call him, so that I may know from which tribe he is, and if he is faithful to go with you." Tobiah went out and called him. Raphael went to Tobit and said to him: "Peace be upon you." Tobit said: "What peace can there be for me? Why has 
<margin>10</margin> all this happened to me? For I do not see the light of heaven. I hear the sound of words, but the man I do not see; I lie in darkness." Raphael said: "God is able to heal your eyes, for you are a pious man." Tobit said to him: "Tobiah my son desires to go to Media. Can you go with him? And we will give you your wages." He said to him: "I can; I am a traveler <note>original: גברא שליחא — literally 'a sent man' or 'messenger'.</note> and I know the paths, the borders, and the mountains." Tobit said: "Tell me, from which 
<margin>15</margin> tribe are you, and from what city do you dwell?" Raphael said: "If I am not suitable in your eyes, go and seek another man who will go with your son." Tobit said: "My brother, do not be angry with me because I desire to know in truth your name and from which family you come." He said to him: "I am Azaria, the son of Hananel, from the house of the great Shemaiah <note>original: שלמיה — likely referring to Shemaiah or Selemias.</note>, one of your brothers." And Tobit said: 
<margin>20</margin> "May you come in quietness and peace, my brother; do not be angry with me for wanting to know your family. Behold, my brother, you are from a good family. I knew Hananel and Nathan, the two sons of the great Shemaiah; they used to go up with me to Jerusalem when we lived in the land of Israel, and they worshipped there with me. They did not stray after those who went astray. You are from a good family; enter in peace. I will give you your wages, a drachma <note>original: טרפעיקא — a 'trach' or drachma, a silver coin.</note> every day, and your food shall be the same as my son's; 
and I will also add to your wages." Raphael answered: "Do not fear, for I will go with your son, and we shall return."

<summary>In this passage, Tobit interviews the angel Raphael (disguised as Azaria) to ensure he is a trustworthy guide for Tobiah’s journey to Media. Tobit lament’s his blindness but is comforted by the angel and agrees to pay him a daily wage for the journey.</summary>

At that time, Tobit remembered the money that he had deposited in the hand of Gabael in Rages, in the land of Media. And he said in his heart: “Behold, I have asked for my soul to die; let me call my son Tobiah and inform him of the matter of the money before I die.” And he called Tobiah his son and said to him: “My son, when I die, bury me with honor. Honor your mother and do not leave her all the days of her life; do for her all that is right in her eyes and do not rebel against the word of her mouth. Remember how she was distressed for you <sup>2</sup> and when she dies, bury her with me in a single grave. 

And all your days, fear the Lord your God, and do not desire to sin, and do not transgress His commandments. For if you act truthfully, it will be well for you in all that is yours; and all doers of charity are blessed.

My son, as long as there is power in your hand to do charity, do it. Even if wealth is far from you, do charity and you will acquire property. And if you do charity, you will acquire a good <term>security</term> <sup>3</sup> <note>The Aramaic 'drufteq' is a loanword from the Greek 'hypotheke,' meaning a legal pledge or deposit.</note> for the day of wrath; for it saves from death and does not allow its master to descend into <sup>4</sup> good darkness. And everyone who does it will be established because of it. For did not our fathers merit praise <sup>5</sup> only through charity? Concerning Abraham our father, the Scripture explains: *‘For I have known him, to the end that he may command his children and his household after him... to do charity and justice’* <note>Genesis 18:19</note>. ### CH. III. HISTORY OF TOBIT.

And You, Judge of all the earth, do not take vengeance upon me for my sins and the sins of my ancestors. For I and my ancestors have sinned before You and turned away from Your commandments. You have given us over to captivity, to plunder, to shame, and to a proverb among all the nations where You have scattered us. And now, O God, Your mercies are many and Your judgment is truth; do not deal with me according to my sins, but according to Your great kindness act <margin>5</margin> toward me. Take my life from my hands, for death is better for me than life, because of this great poverty and these reproaches; let me not hear reproach anymore. 

On that same day, Sarah the daughter of Raguel, who was in Ecbatana <note>original: באגבתנים (Agvatanim) — the capital of Media, modern-day Hamadan, Iran.</note>, a city in the land of Media, heard a great reproach. This was because she had been given to seven men as a wife, but no man had come to her according to the custom of all the earth <note>A common euphemism for the consummation of marriage.</note>; for Ashmodai <note>original: אשמדאי (Ashmodai) — a king of demons in Jewish folklore, often associated with the destruction of marriages.</note>, the king of demons, killed them before they could come to her as is the custom of <margin>10</margin> all the earth. 

Her maidservant said to her: “It is you who have killed your husbands! For you have been given to seven men, and not one of them has come to you, yet you beat us! Because of the men you killed, may you die just as they died, and may we never see from you a son or a daughter forever!” 

When Sarah heard these words, she was greatly distressed and wept. She went up to her father’s upper chamber and intended to hang herself <note>original: למצלב גרמה — literally "to impale/hang herself," a reference to suicide by hanging.</note> <margin>15</margin> but then she thought of the sorrow it would bring to her father, bringing his gray hair down in grief to Sheol. <note>original: לשאול (Sheol) — the biblical place of the dead, often translated as the grave or the underworld.</note> <sup>1</sup> Therefore, she said: “It is not good for me to hang myself, but it is better to pray before God so that I do not hear reproach anymore.” 

At that time, she spread out her hands in prayer before God and said: “Blessed are You, O Lord, Merciful and Gracious God! Blessed is Your holy and distinct Name which is declared throughout all ages! May all Your handiwork bless You forever and ever! And now, O Lord, before You I have lifted my face and my eyes <margin>20</margin> hang toward You. Say the word that I may return to the dust and not hear my reproaches anymore. It is revealed before You, Lord, that I am pure from all the uncleanness of man, and I have not defiled my name <sup>2</sup> or the name of my father in the land of my exile. I am the only child to my father, and he has no other son to inherit his possession, nor does he have a close relative who might take me as a wife. Yet seven men have already died because of me! What life is there for me anymore? If it is not good in Your sight to kill me, have pity on me and [do not let me hear reproach anymore]...”

### 2

And it happened when the Feast of Weeks <note>original: חגא דשבועיא (Chaga de-Shavuaya) — also known as Pentecost, a harvest festival.</note> arrived, he prepared a great banquet. And when he sat at the table, he said to Tobit his son: "Go and bring for me some of our poor brothers from those who fear God, to eat with us; and I will wait for you until you return." And Tobit went and found a man who had been killed and cast in the street, and he told his father. And when <margin>5</margin> Tobit heard this, he rose from the table and did not eat; he went and took him from the street of the city and brought him into a house until the sun should set, so that he could bury him. And he returned to his house and ate his bread in mourning and sorrow, and said: "Woe, for there has been fulfilled in us the prophecy: 'I will turn your feasts into mourning'" <note>A citation of Amos 8:10.</note>; and he wept with a very great weeping. And when the sun set, he went and buried him. His relatives mocked him, saying: "Does this man not fear for his life? <margin>10</margin> For he was sought for execution for this very thing, yet he still buries the dead!" 

And on that night, he did not wash after touching the dead <note>Referring to ritual purification laws</note>, and he lay on his bed by the wall with his face uncovered. And he did not know that there were birds <note>Often identified as sparrows in other versions.</note> above him on the wall, and their droppings fell upon his eyes, and a film formed over his eyes. And every morning he went to the physician<sup>1</sup> to heal his eyes, but he was not healed; rather, the whiteness increased in his eyes until he became blind. And he was blind for four years. And all his brothers and relatives were grieved for him, and Ahiqar <margin>15</margin> <note>original: אקיקר (Akikar) — a famous legendary sage, identified here as Tobit’s nephew.</note> supported him. 

After many days, Hannah his wife did work for other women, and they gave her a kid goat as her wages. And he heard the kid bleating in the house and asked her: "From where did you get this kid? Perhaps it is stolen? Return it to its owner, for it is not right for us to eat from a stolen thing." She answered him: "It is not from theft; <margin>20</margin> rather, I received it as the wage for my handiwork." But Tobit did not believe the matter and argued with her about the kid. Then Hannah his wife answered and said to him: "Where are your good deeds and your righteousness? Your shame is now revealed to all!"

### 3

When Tobit heard this, he was greatly distressed and wept, and he began to pray in the anguish of his soul, and thus<sup>2</sup> he said: "You are righteous, O Great God; all Your works are mighty, and all Your ways are kindness and truth."

<header>CH. I. HISTORY OF TOBIT.</header>

Tobit was taken captive and lived in Nineveh <note>The ancient capital of the Assyrian Empire, located in modern-day Iraq.</note>, the great city. All his brothers and kinsmen defiled themselves and ate the bread of the Gentiles <note>original: בני עממיא (bene ammaya) — "sons of the peoples." This refers to breaking Jewish dietary laws by eating food prepared by non-Jews.</note>, but he did not eat, for he feared God and loved Him with all his heart. Because of this, God granted him favor and kindness in the eyes of Shalmaneser, the king of Assyria, who appointed him as an overseer over everything he possessed <margin>5</margin> until the day of his death. At that time, Tobit deposited ten talents of silver <note>A talent was a significant unit of weight for currency; ten talents represented a massive fortune.</note> in the trust of his brother Gabael in the land of Media, in the city of Rages. 

When King Shalmaneser of Assyria died, his son Sennacherib reigned in his place. In those days, the forced labor <term>corvée: mandatory state labor or high taxes often imposed on conquered peoples</term> increased, and Tobit was no longer able to travel to the land of Media because the roads were blocked by those who travel the paths <note>referring to highwaymen or military blockades</note> due to the instability; thus, he could not retrieve his money from the hand of Gabael. 

In the days of Sennacherib, <margin>10</margin> Tobit performed many righteous deeds for the needy. He would feed the hungry and the orphans, clothe the naked, and practice great acts of loving-kindness. Whenever he saw the corpse of a slain Jew cast out in the street, he would bury him. 

When Sennacherib returned to Nineveh in shame from Judah <note>A reference to the miraculous defeat of the Assyrian army outside Jerusalem described in 2 Kings 19.</note>, he went in a great rage against the ten tribes in the land of Assyria and killed many of them. Their corpses were left lying in the street with no one to bury them. When Tobit saw this, it grieved him deeply; <margin>15</margin> he rose up in the night, stole their bodies, and buried them. He did this many times. 

On one occasion, Sennacherib sought the bodies of the slain but did not find them. Some people of Nineveh went and informed <note>original: ואכלו קורציה (ve-akhlu kurtzeih) — literally "ate his pieces," an Aramaic idiom meaning to slander or accuse someone maliciously.</note> the king that it was Tobit who was burying them. The king ordered that he be killed. When Tobit heard this, he rose and fled. The king then ordered that his house be plundered. Tobit remained hidden from him for forty-five days, until Adrammelech and Sharezer, the king's own sons, killed him <note>Sennacherib</note> with the sword. <margin>20</margin> They escaped to the land of Kardu <note>The mountains of Ararat.</note>, and Esarhaddon his son reigned in his place. 

King Esarhaddon appointed Achiacharus <note>The nephew of Tobit, a famous figure in ancient Near Eastern wisdom literature.</note>, the son of his brother Hamal, over all his affairs, and he was the ruler over all the land of Assyria. Achiacharus spoke good words on behalf of Tobit to the king and petitioned for him, and thus he brought him back to Nineveh, because Achiacharus was his friend and kinsman. In that time <note>original: ובאנונה (u-be-anunah) — The manuscript has a scribal variation here, likely referring to the same time period.</note>, they restored to him Anna his wife and Tobias his son.

---

¹ So MS., and also on p. 9, l. 22. <note>A translator's note regarding the specific spelling of "Anna" or the time period in the original manuscript.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Tobit as a righteous man in the Assyrian captivity who risks his life to bury the dead and help the poor. It details his escape from King Sennacherib's wrath and his eventual return to Nineveh through the influence of his nephew Achiacharus.</summary>

# I. CHALDEE TEXT <note>original: "Chaldee" is an older term for the Aramaic language.</note>

## ⁕ THE STORY OF TOBIT ⁕

And it is written in the *Midrash Rabbah* <note>A collection of ancient Jewish homiletic interpretations of the Bible.</note> of the Great, in the section "And Jacob went out" <note>Genesis 28:10</note>, section 70, regarding the verse: **"And of all that You give me, I will surely give a tenth to You."** <note>Genesis 28:22</note> **"You shall surely tithe"** <note>Deuteronomy 14:22</note>—Moses said to them: "Ten blessings you shall receive if you tithe." And so Jacob said: **"And of all that You give me, I will surely give a tenth to You"**—ten blessings You will give me, just as my father blessed me. By what merit? By the merit of <margin>5</margin> **"I will surely give a tenth to You."**

->1<-

There was a story of a certain pious man named Tobit, son of Tobiel, from the tribe of Naphtali, who was taken captive in the days of Shalmaneser, King of Assyria. He lived in Thisbe, a city of Naphtali in Galilee. All his days, Tobit walked in the upright way and performed many good deeds for his brothers and his people who were with him in the captivity in Nineveh in the land of Assyria. And when he was <margin>10</margin> a young man in the land of Israel, the entire tribe of Naphtali rebelled against the kingdom of David and prevented themselves from going up to Jerusalem. They would sacrifice to the [golden] calves that Jeroboam, King of Israel, made in Bethel and in Dan. But he alone went to Jerusalem at the times of the festivals, as it is written in the book of the Torah of Moses. He would bring there the firstfruits, the offerings, and the tithes, and give them to the priests and the Levites—to each and every one what was due to him. And the second tithe and the poor tithe he would eat and give according to everything written in the book of Moses. This Tobit was left an orphan by his father, and Deborah, his father’s mother, raised him; she guided him in the straight path. When he became a man, he took a wife from his own lineage <note>original: מזריעתה (mizriateh) — "from his seed," meaning from his own tribe or kin.</note> named Hannah, and she bore him a son, and he called his name Tobiah. When...

<summary>This page introduces Tobit as a pious man from the tribe of Naphtali living in Assyrian captivity, emphasizing his faithfulness to Jerusalem and tithing laws despite his tribe's apostasy. It establishes his lineage and his early life as an orphan raised by his grandmother, Deborah.</summary>
